Acknowledgement of Country
The Wheatbelt Development Commission 
respectfully acknowledges the Traditional Owners 
of the lands on which we live and work, being 
the family and clan groups of the Ballardong, 
Goreng, Gubrun, Kalamaia, Njaki-Njaki, Whadjuk, 
Wiilman, Wudjari, Yued and Yamatji Peoples of the 
Noongar Nation. We pay our respects to Elders 
past, present and emerging, and recognise their 
enduring connection to Country, founded on deep 
knowledge, stewardship and culture.

5
The Noongar Nation peoples are made up of fourteen different language 
groups each correlated with different geographic areas and unique cultural 
knowledge (Kaartdijin) across south-west Western Australia:
Amangu, Yued/Yuat, Whadjuk/Wajuk, Binjareb/Pinjarup, Wardandi,  
Balardong/Ballardong, Nyakinyaki, Wilman, Ganeang, Bibulmun/Piblemen, 
Mineng, Goreng and Wudjari and Njunga.

Building on the Southern 
Wheatbelt Regional Drought 
Resilience Plan
The Southern Wheatbelt (SW) Regional 
Drought Resilience Plan was developed 
as a standalone plan in 2021–22 as 
part of the pilot phase of the RDRP 
Program in Western Australia. The SW 
RDRP encompasses 28,512km2 across 
the Shires of Dumbleyung, Wagin, Lake 
Grace, Kulin and Kondinin. A drought 
vulnerability assessment deemed the 
region highly to very highly 
vulnerable to drought. 
The SW RDRP identified five key themes — Resilient Water, Resilient Communities, 
Resilient Agriculture, Resilient Landscapes and Resilient Regional Economies, 
and over 130 interventions to build drought resilience. 
Notably, SW stakeholders identify improving water system resilience and reliability 
as the key priority to build drought resilience in the region, alongside a desire to 
better articulate agricultural systems research and development needs, building 
strengthened regional connections and project specific opportunities to diversify 
the economy. 
The initial implementation projects concentrate on building resilient water systems, 
by focusing on identifying new water sources and improving water collection  
and storage for community and emergency use.
6

7
7
The Wheatbelt RDRP builds on the work completed in the  
SW RDRP. The Wheatbelt Plan supersedes the SW RDRP, through 
the actions and interventions identified within the plan will be 
ratified through incorporation into the whole of Wheatbelt plan. 
Southern Wheatbelt stakeholders should note the broader regional and  
sub-regional actions in the Wheatbelt RDRP are relevant to the Southern Wheatbelt region, 
supporting continuity and integration across both planning areas.
Southern Wheatbelt Regional 
Drought Resilience Plan
Wheatbelt Development Commission

9
Foreword
In a drying climate, it is no longer viable to wait for drought before taking 
action. Resilience must be built ahead of time through the way we plan, invest 
and make decisions about infrastructure, services and community readiness.
The Wheatbelt is a region of strategic and economic importance to WA. It 
contributes more than one third of the State’s agricultural output, produces 
over half of the large-scale wind and solar energy in the South West 
Interconnected System, and serves as a central corridor for regional and 
national supply chains. 
However, these strengths are closely tied to climate conditions. Declining 
rainfall, reduced streamflow, shorter growing seasons and more frequent 
heat extremes are placing pressure on community wellbeing, infrastructure 
and regional economies. Preparing for these conditions is essential to 
protecting liveability and building longer term resilience.
and the WA Government, the Wheatbelt Regional Drought Resilience Plan 
(the Plan) draws on the knowledge and experience of those who live and 
work in the region, supported by scientific research and climate data. It 
examines the social, economic and environmental impacts of drought and 
provides a roadmap for strengthening preparedness, guiding investment and 
supporting more resilient communities over time.
Importantly, the Plan reflects the capabilities that already exist within 
the Wheatbelt. Community leadership, innovation, agricultural expertise 
and strong social networks provide a foundation for collective action and 
practical solutions that support long term resilience.
I would like to acknowledge the many local governments, community groups, 
industry representatives and individuals who contributed their experience, 
ideas and insight to shape this Plan. Your input has ensured it reflects the 
diverse needs, opportunities and aspirations of the region.
The Wheatbelt Development Commission looks forward to continuing these 
partnerships as we move from planning to action and build a more resilient 
future for our region.
Rob Cossart
Chief Executive Officer
Wheatbelt Development Commission

10
Executive 
Summary
The Wheatbelt Regional Drought 
Resilience Plan (RDRP) outlines a 
two-pronged approach to strengthening 
resilience across the Wheatbelt. 
It addresses place-based challenge 
while advancing longer-term, 
region-wide strategies to manage 
complex drought risks. The plan 
is underpinned by foundational 
actions that support communication, 
monitoring and analysis, providing 
a strong evidence base for targeted 
investment and action.
Image courtesy: Shire of Northam
The RDRP provides a coordinated, risk-based framework to strengthen drought 
Drought Resilience Planning principles, the plan focuses on building the capacity of 
communities, industries and landscapes to anticipate, absorb and adapt to drought, 
while maintaining economic productivity, environmental condition and social wellbeing. 
The Wheatbelt is a vast and diverse region of 77,000 people 
across 42 local government authorities (LGAs), ranging from 
large regional centres to very small towns. The regional 
economy is stable, generating a gross regional product of 
$9.12 billion, underpinned by strong grains and mining sectors 
and supported by 9,500 small and medium enterprises.
10

11
Despite this strength, the region faces growing climate, economic and 
demographic pressures that vary across its five sub-regions. Building  
long-term drought resilience will require collective regional action, 
strengthened local capacity and forward-looking planning across social, 
natural and built systems. 
Developed during 2024–25, the Wheatbelt RDRP is informed by 
a comprehensive drought vulnerability assessment and extensive 
engagement with regional stakeholders. It identifies six interlinked 
themes that together form the foundation of resilience across the region:

13
Water security 
Consistent with national drought resilience priorities, water security is 
identified as a key driver of Wheatbelt drought resilience. In some areas, 
population growth and emerging industries are intensifying competition 
for limited groundwater. In others, declining rainfall and ageing 
infrastructure are reducing the reliability of traditional water supplies, 
prompting investigation of alternative water sources and delivery models. 
This variability highlights the need for tailored, place based solutions that 
reflect local conditions and future demand.
Agricultural and horticultural resilience
Broadacre agriculture remains the Wheatbelt’s dominant industry, 
contributing 25% of the region’s annual economic value and 30% of 
Western Australia’s agricultural output. The sector supports 30% of 
employment and 45% of local businesses. While this largely rain-fed 
sector has adapted well to a hotter, drier climate - with grain yields 
increasing over the past 50 years despite a 20% decline in annual rainfall 
- pressures are intensifying. Yield gains are narrowing, seasonal variability 
is increasing, and farm dams are less reliable during consecutive dry 
years. Continued, locally focused research, development and extension is 
required to support long term productivity, particularly through improved 
water-use efficiency and securing on-farm water supplies.
There is also significant opportunity to improve water-use efficiency 
among the Wheatbelt’s more water intensive horticultural and livestock 
enterprises. The irrigated horticultural sector generates around 15% of 
WA’s horticultural value and accounts for the majority of groundwater use 
in the fast-growing Central Coast sub-region. Increasing competition for 
groundwater, combined with declining rainfall and recharge underscores 
the need for improved strategic planning to better understand current 
resources and future demand.
Community resilience
While drought resilience and climate change adaptation have received 
strong attention at the farm level through Federal, State and local initiatives, 
comparatively less work has focused on community level vulnerability and 
resilience. Community resilience across the Wheatbelt is complex1, and 
further work is required to develop measurable social resilience indicators.
Self-reliance and local initiatives are defining strengths of Wheatbelt 
communities, supported by the largest volunteer workforce in Western 
Australia. However, volunteer burnout is a growing risk, exacerbated by 
drought and climate pressures that affect mental health, water supplies, 
recreational facilities, and the reliability of power and telecommunications. 
An ageing and slow-growing population is placing additional pressure on 
volunteer supply at the same time as demand increases. Strengthened 
capacity-building and strategic planning at local government and 
community levels are essential to support volunteers and build long-term 
resilience.
Volunteers underpin sporting, social and emergency 
services across the Wheatbelt, but an ageing,  
slow-growing population is placing pressure on both 
volunteer supply and demand. 
1. ‘Living Well in the Wheatbelt’ strategy (2025–2035) Wheatbelt District Leadership Group

14
Natural environment resilience
Extensive land clearing since the early 1900s has made the Wheatbelt one of 
Australia’s most productive cropping regions, but in some LGAs less than 5% 
of original native vegetation remains. Highly fragmented remnant woodlands 
now support over 200 threatened species, with the region forming part of one 
of 36 globally recognised biodiversity hotspots. 
These natural assets hold deep cultural significance for Noongar Traditional 
Owners, are central to community wellbeing, and underpin a nature-based 
tourism economy generating around $400 million annually.  Sites most at risk 
from the combined impacts of dry seasons, shallow soils and groundwater 
depletion should be prioritised for monitoring. Landholders, Aboriginal ranger 
teams and community groups need support to protect and buffer high risk 
and ecologically critical remnant vegetation. There are also opportunities to 
target global interest in land based natural environment investment to  
vulnerable areas.
Building drought resilience: anticipate,  
absorb and adapt
The Wheatbelt RDRP adopts a nationally consistent resilience framework 
Anticipate
Improving the capacity to anticipate drought risk requires access to reliable 
data and consistent indicators to build strong situational awareness. The 
Wheatbelt region is assessed as ‘moderately vulnerable’ overall, with some 
areas more highly exposed due to declining rainfall and reliance on rain 
fed agriculture. Further work is required to establish a more detailed and 
accurate baseline of drought vulnerability and resilience, particularly at 
LGA and sub-regional levels across water, infrastructure, human capital and 
natural environment. Foundational actions identified through stakeholder 
engagement will strengthen evidence-based planning and better inform 
decision making.
Implementing the foundational activities identified 
through the Wheatbelt RDRP stakeholder engagement 
process will strengthen measures of drought 
vulnerability and enable more accurate assessment of 
drought resilience over time, supporting tailored policy 
development and action across the region.
A drought resilient Wheatbelt is one in which communities, landscapes 
and industries can anticipate, absorb, and adapt to drought conditions 
while maintaining wellbeing, economic stability, and environmental 
sustainability.

15
Foundational drought resilience activities identified through the Wheatbelt RDRP include:
• 	 Communicate the Wheatbelt RDRP strategies through targeted engagement and partner alignment.
• 	 Undertake community and sub-regional water needs analyses.
• 	 Support LGAs to assess priority infrastructure needs.
• 	 Review and update sub-regional economic and growth plans to reflect climate risks.
• 	 Developing an online platform for carbon, biodiversity and offset investment.
• 	 Auditing community green space to guide improved management.
Image courtesy: Kulin Bush Races

16
16
Absorb
Reducing the impacts of drought requires strong communities, resilient 
infrastructure, diversified economies and well managed natural systems. 
Pressures on volunteers, ageing infrastructure, water reliability and 
fragmented landscapes heighten vulnerability during prolonged dry periods. 
Adapt
Adaptation is an ongoing process that requires continual learning, innovation 
and coordinated regional governance. Across the Wheatbelt, communities and 
industries are already demonstrating strong adaptive capability—from trialling 
desalination and re-engineering dams to expanding the land restoration 
economy and developing collaborative housing and service models. The region 
has a solid foundation of collaborative governance, though sustaining capacity 
requires ongoing attention and support. Collaborative approaches are critical 
to the region’s ability to adapt and innovate to remain resilient and continue to 
prosper in a changing climate with increasing drought risk.
The remoteness and small size of many Wheatbelt 
communities has resulted in the region’s towns having 
an embedded quality of self-sufficiency and social 
connectedness.
The RDRP identifies a mix of immediate and longer-term 
actions to reduce exposure an mitigate future impacts:
• 	 Implementing the ‘Living Well in the Wheatbelt’ strategy.
• 	 Deliver a ‘Resilient Wheatbelt’ small grants program.
• 	 Support small business climate resilience.
• 	 Building leadership capacity through existing networks and 
programs.
• 	 Embedding climate scenario planning in regional plans.
• 	 Ensure horticulture growth and climate adaptation plans have 
a focus on the Central Coast and Central Midlands region.
• 	 Strengthen cross regional agricultural networks.
Image courtesy: Williams CRC

17
Region wide adaptation priorities include:
• 	 Regularly update  the Wheatbelt DVA with new data and 
local insights.
• 	 Establishing a Wheatbelt water working group to facilitate 
collective water planning and advocacy across potable and 
non-potable supplies.
• 	 Build on existing sub-regional structures to establish 
a regional planning group to strengthen coordinated 
infrastructure planning and advocacy across.
• 	 Supporting innovative workforce and training models to 
support cross sector skills development. 
• 	 Expanding the nature based and land restoration economy.
• 	 Sharing emergency management learning across 
communities.

18
18
A living resilience framework
The Wheatbelt RDRP outlines more than 30 priority actions across 
foundational, quick win and future pathway categories (Section 10).  
They capture the needs of a region experiencing record grain production, 
while also facing significant infrastructure and capacity challenges to 
manage growth, protect environmental health and maintain essential 
services. Figure 1 demonstrates how these actions are linked within the 
action plan framework. 
Designed as a living document, the Wheatbelt RDRP 
will be reviewed annually and updated every five years, 
to reflect new data, shifting economic conditions and 
evolving community and industry needs. 
The plan provides a long-term framework for coordinated investment, policy 
development and project delivery, supporting sustained economic, social and 
environmental resilience to drought across the Wheatbelt.

20
Introduction
Building long-lasting 
resilience across the 
Wheatbelt will require 
a multi-pronged and 
collaborative approach — one 
that recognises the need for 
ongoing innovation across 
agricultural and horticultural 
enterprises in the face of 
climate change while also 
(and importantly) attending 
to the social, economic and 
environmental vulnerabilities 
faced by the region’s many 
and far-spread communities.
The Wheatbelt Regional Drought Resilience Plan (the Wheatbelt RDRP) is the culmination of in-depth 
data collection and analysis along with hundreds of discussions held across the region during 2024 
and 2025 years. Notably, this plan was developed in the year that the Wheatbelt’s major industry, 
broadacre agriculture, achieved record-breaking grain yields.
Through the course of developing the Wheatbelt RDRP it became  
evident that despite a sophisticated understanding of the impacts 
of climate change and drought on rain-fed broadacre enterprises, 
significant gaps exist in quantifying and monitoring the environmental 
and social impacts of drought to enable a true measure of adaptive 
capacity across the Wheatbelt.
To better understand these gaps in knowledge and determine what was needed to address 
them, assessments of water supply and demand and the status of communications and power 
infrastructure, road networks, housing supply, natural environment and economic diversity  
were undertaken at local and sub-regional levels across the Wheatbelt. 
Image courtesy:  Angie Roe, Austockphoto

21
21
Underpinning this work was a comprehensive stakeholder consultation 
process in which more than 300 people representing over 200 different 
groups and organisations were interviewed in-person, online or by phone 
using a structured format to gather qualitative information on the drought 
resilience issues and barriers facing the Wheatbelt and the short- and long-
term action needed to build resilience across communities, industries and 
local government.
In addition, case studies were developed to provide a snapshot of the 
resilience already embedded across the region.
The Wheatbelt RDRP Steering Committee collated and prioritised the 
stakeholder consultation responses over 20 hours of in-person workshops 
and online discussions into a prioritised list of activities that were  deemed a 
‘quick win’ project, longer-term ‘future pathways’ strategy or a ‘foundational’ 
activity. This prioritisation process formed the basis for the Wheatbelt RDRP 
implementation plan (a summary of which can be found in Section 10 of the 
document). Prioritised drought resilience activities are identified across 
six implementation themes and their underlying outcomes collectively 
capturing the main drivers of drought resilience within the region’s 
communities, economies and landscapes (Figure 2).

Reading the Wheatbelt RDRP
The Wheatbelt RDRP is divided into 10 sections (see Figure 3). Sections 
1–2 provide a detailed overview of the Wheatbelt — its size, sub-regions, 
people, industries, economies, and climate. Sections 3 defines drought 
and resilience for the Wheatbelt whilst Section 4 assesses economic, 
environmental and social data for the Wheatbelt to provide a region-wide 
drought vulnerability assessment.
Sections 5–9 are in-depth discussions of how drought and climate change 
have impacted the Wheatbelt’s primary industries, water supply and demand, 
communities and sub-regions and how projected climate change is expected 
to play out across the region. Case studies in this section showcase how 
Wheatbelt communities and sub-regions are responding to the impacts of a 
drier, more variable climate. These stories highlight the resilience embedded 
across the region and act as examples of what is possible with collective 
action, collaborative governance and local leadership. 
Section 10 collates and integrates the findings of the stakeholder 
engagement interviews and the quantitative and qualitative analyses done 
during the Wheatbelt RDRP process — into an implementation plan that 
provides a framework for building sustained drought resilience  
across the Wheatbelt.
Hundreds of hours of discussions and analysis informed the Wheatbelt 
RDRP — a plan that has been developed from the ‘ground up’ to capture the 
practical and fit-for-purpose action that Wheatbelt communities want and 
need to build resilience across their region. 
The Wheatbelt RDRP is offered as a valuable resource 
for use by communities, local governments, industry 
groups and government agencies to inform decision 
making, attract funding and guide investment in 
sustained drought resilience on behalf of the 77,000 
people who call the Wheatbelt home.

1. Wheatbelt Region — overview
29
• 	 Size 155,000 km2 (two-thirds the size of Victoria) 
• 	 Population 77,000 people with some areas to the west growing at considerable rates
• 	 Economy based on agriculture and mining but growing in diversity (renewables, 
manufacturing)
• 	 Climate rainfall from 600 mm in the west to less than 300 mm in the east. One of the most 
affected areas globally in terms of rainfall decline from climate change.
• 	 Environment the Wheatbelt sits within the South-West Botanical Province of Western 
Australia — one of just 36 globally-recognised biodiversity hotspots.
• 	 Drought vulnerability economic diversification, capacity building and strategic planning 
needed to build resilience across the region
The Wheatbelt Region at a glance:

1. Wheatbelt Region — overview
30
30
Introduction
Introduction
The Wheatbelt of 
Western Australia 
covers more than 
155,000 square 
kilometres and is home 
to about 77,000 people 
disbursed across five 
sub-regions in 200+ 
local communities 
administered by 42 local 
governments (LGAs). 
The Wheatbelt’s five sub-regions — Avon, Wheatbelt South, Central Coast, Central East and Central 
Midlands, span from the Indian Ocean in the west to the edge of the arid Goldfields in the east and from the 
border of WA’s pastoral region in the north down to beef, dairy and wine country in the south (Figure 1). For 
ease of analysis the five Southern Wheatbelt RDRP LGAs are included in the Wheatbelt South sub-region.
“
“Resilience is about being healthy and well across the lifespan… Our 
communities and the people in them need to be given the opportunity to 
start well, grow well, work well, live well and age well.”

The Wheatbelt is one 
of nine WA regions and 
spans an area two-thirds 
the size of Victoria.

1. Wheatbelt Region — overview
Wheatbelt town populations range from a high of more than 11,000 (Northam) 
through to less than 200, with some smaller settlements home to fewer than 
a dozen people. A few Wheatbelt towns are just under an hour from the Perth 
metro but most require a journey of two to four hours.
Despite having one of the strongest economies in Western Australia, more 
than half the Wheatbelt’s population has a socio-economic score less than the 
state average.
About 90% of the region was cleared for agriculture about 100 years ago and 
the remaining 10% of remnant vegetation holds a high conservation value 
that is recognised both globally and nationally. Many of the plant and animal 
species in the Wheatbelt are found nowhere else in the world and the region’s 
150 kilometres of coastline north of Perth is a significant tourism area. 
Just over half of the Wheatbelt is arable land dedicated to broadacre 
agriculture, horticulture and intensive livestock production with the region 
contributing 30% of WA’s total agricultural value in 2023–2024. Annual rainfall 
in the region ranges from 600 mm in the west to less than half this (295 mm) in 
the east (Figure 2). 
Water for towns, farms and communities comes from the many millions of 
litres of fresh groundwater abstracted from beneath the Central Coast  
sub-region each year through to the few hundred millimetres of annual rainfall 
that falls in the Central East. Inland areas are serviced by the piped water 
scheme from Perth.
Climate change is impacting the amount and timing of annual rainfall across 
the region with concomitant impacts on ground and surface water supplies. 
Each Wheatbelt sub-region faces a unique set of water challenges and creating 
sustainable drought resilience in the Wheatbelt will require tailored approaches. 
The Wheatbelt is a vast and diverse region that requires an equally diverse drought 
resilience response — one with a local focus encompassed by a strategic regional overview.

1. Wheatbelt Region — overview
Wheatbelt — economy
Total economic output 
from the Wheatbelt in 
2024 was just under 
$18 billion (Figure 3). 
Agriculture and mining account for a combined 50% of the Wheatbelt’s economic output but agriculture 
accounts for nearly half of all businesses in the region (compared to <1% for mining) and is responsible for 
four times as many jobs.
Agriculture is the largest employer across all Wheatbelt sub-regions and about 45% of all Wheatbelt 
businesses are connected to the industry (Figure 4). The construction sector accounts for the second largest 
share of Wheatbelt businesses (11%). 
Building drought resilience across the Wheatbelt will require continued 
climate adaptation in the agriculture sector along with economic 
diversification into sectors less reliant on seasonal rainfall.

The Avon is the most diversified of the Wheatbelt’s sub-regions, followed by 
the Central Coast while the Central East is a significant player in the mining 
industry, accounting for about 60% of the Wheatbelt’s current mining value 
(Figure 5). 
Continued diversification of the Wheatbelt’s economy will be critical to 
building drought resilience across the region and reducing its vulnerability to 
dry seasons and climate change.
Economic analysis by the Bankwest Curtin Economic 
Centre (2025)2 found the Wheatbelt’s diversification 
index reflects a moderate level of economic diversity 
with an obvious main strength in agriculture and 
comparative advantages in mining, wholesaling 
(grain), and manufacturing/construction. The report 
concludes that the region has significant potential (and 
imperative) to diversify into emerging sectors such as 
renewable energy and critical mineral mining as well 
as downstream processing of agricultural and mining 
commodities, transportation logistics and tourism.

for the Wheatbelt Development Commission (unpublished)

1. Wheatbelt Region — overview
40
Wheatbelt  
— population 
The Wheatbelt has the 
third highest population 
(˜77,000) of all WA 
regions but the region 
as a whole is growing at 
one of the slowest rates 
— with total population 
increasing by just 2.3% 
between 2015 and 2023 
compared to 13.4% 
across WA (Figure 6). 
Population is highly dispersed across the Wheatbelt’s 200+ communities ranging from more than 11,000 
(2021) in the regional centre of Northam (Avon sub-region) through to fewer than 200 in smaller and more 
remote communities. Population dynamics vary widely at a sub-regional level with Avon and Central Coast 
experiencing significant population growth between 2015 and 2023 (Figure 7). 
Between 2015 and 2023 the Central Coast sub-region grew at 15.4% — faster 
than all other regions in WA except for Peel (23.9%) (Figures 6 & 7).
The Wheatbelt has a significantly older population relative to the state average with a median age of  
47 compared to 40.6 for the WA average.3 In 2021, people aged 55–64 made up the highest proportion 
(16%) of the Wheatbelt’s population compared with 12% for WA as a whole (Figure 8).

1. Wheatbelt Region — overview
Rural communities need to maintain a critical number of residents in a range of 
demographics to remain viable and attract services and infrastructure. 
Population decline in some areas of the Wheatbelt could 
be further exacerbated by a drying climate. Economic 
diversification (renewable energy, downstream processing of 
agricultural and mining commodities, tourism development) 
and investment in and advocacy for infrastructure 
development and service delivery in Wheatbelt communities 
will be needed to build drought resilience and maintain and 
grow populations across the region.
In other areas — such as west Avon and Central Coast — growing populations 
are placing higher demand on water supplies that will come under increasing 
threat as the climate dries. Water supply and demand audits and investment in 
infrastructure and services are needed to underpin this population growth  
and build resilience as the climate dries.

2. Wheatbelt Region — climate 
Introduction
Western Australia’s 
southwest (which 
includes the Wheatbelt) 
has experienced 
climate-induced drying 
at one of the fastest 
rates in the world, and 
this is projected to 
continue.1
Climate change is affecting every community in the state. People living 
in rural and remote regions face additional challenges because of their 
relative isolation.
Climate Adaptation Strategy — building WA’s climate resilient future (Government of 
Western Australia, 2023)
This section outlines how the climate has changed across the Wheatbelt over the past 50 years and the 
predicted climate change expected out to 2050.
Successful climate adaptation across the Wheatbelt will be underpinned by:
•	 Tailored climate projections communicated at sub-regional and industry levels
•	 Sub-regional and community-level water supply and demand audits
•	 Community-level audits of green spaces, pools, water capture and storage initiatives
•	 Strategic infrastructure planning at community and sub-regional levels
•	 Continued RD&E investment across primary industries
1.	 IPCC, 2021: Climate Change 2021: The Physical Science Basis. Contribution of Working Group I to the Sixth Assessment Report 
of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change, Cambridge University Press

2. Wheatbelt Region — climate 
52
Recent climate
Annual rainfall across 
south-west WA (which 
includes the Wheatbelt) 
has declined by 10–20% 
over the past 50 years. 
As a result, rainfall zones in the Wheatbelt have shifted southwest by 100 km and the area designated as 
low rainfall (<325 mm) has nearly doubled (Figure 1).
The decline in rainfall has resulted in fewer wet years and many more lower rainfall years across the 
Wheatbelt with the traditional ‘break of season’ (defined as 25 mm of rain over three days) becoming later 
and more variable — especially on heavier soils (Figure 2). 
“
“Wheatbelt industries and communities need access to information on 
current and future climate risks to plan for and build climate change 
resilience across the region.”

2. Wheatbelt Region — climate 
55
Over the past 40 years, seasonal drought frequency 
has increased by a factor of 3–4 in some parts of the 
Wheatbelt (Figure 3). 
Over the past 50 years, rainfall decline has resulted in an 80% reduction in 
streamflow into water reservoirs supplying Perth and south-west WA (which 
includes the Wheatbelt) (Figure 4).
With significantly less runoff, many of the 200,000 farm dams across the 
Wheatbelt are capturing only 20–30% of the run-off they captured 40-years 
ago with many no longer meeting farm water demands during consecutive low 
rainfall years (for more information see Section 5 Wheatbelt Water Demand).

2. Wheatbelt Region — climate 
Predicted climate 
2.	 Sudmeyer, R A, Edward, A, Fazakerley, V, Simpkin, L, and Foster, I. (2016), Climate change: impacts and adaptation for agriculture in 
Western Australia. Department of Primary Industries and Regional Development, Western Australia, Perth. Bulletin 4870.
Climate modelling 
predicts that the drying 
trend experienced across 
the Wheatbelt since the 
mid-1970s is likely to 
continue with annual 
rainfall projected to 
decline a further 2–10%, 
particularly in eastern 
areas (Figure 5). 
The climate models have high confidence of less rainfall in winter and spring and that annual rainfall will 
decline, but changes to autumn and summer rainfall remain unclear.
Growing season rainfall is expected to decline further across the Wheatbelt under a medium emissions 
scenario — with the area receiving less than 200mm rainfall expanding to the southwest (Figure 6). 
Crop yield will be most affected by changes in rainfall, particularly timing of rainfall. Declining growing 
season rainfall and lighter rainfall events will make evaporation losses more significant, and less water 
will be stored deep in the soil. Plant available water capacity of soils will become increasingly important 
in determining yield, with yield declines likely to be greater on clay soils compared to sands, particularly 
in drier eastern areas. Crop yields may be largely unchanged or increase in wetter western and southern 
areas where higher temperatures and reduced waterlogging will benefit crops.2
By 2050 under a medium emissions scenario, there will be 20–30% more 
days above 35°C across the Wheatbelt — resulting in increased heat stress in 
communities, crops and livestock (Figure 7).

Higher temperatures, and to a lesser extent declining rainfall, will hasten 
crop development times and reduce the flowering period. Each day with 
temperatures above 35°C during grain fill can reduce wheat yield by 5% 
while temperatures above 32°C during canola flowering can cause flower 
abortion.3 In extreme temperatures, sheep and cattle can require up to 
80% more water and reproductive rates can drop.4,5,6

to the coastal strip. Its impacts are wide-ranging, 
extending beyond farmers and their families to

3.  Defining drought and drought resilience for the Wheatbelt

3.  Defining drought and drought resilience for the Wheatbelt
67
• 	 A prolonged period of abnormally dry conditions that impacts negatively on water 
availability and agricultural production in a region and, consequently, impacts negatively on 
the economy and environment of the region and the health and wellbeing of its residents 
• 	 A drought resilient Wheatbelt is one in which communities, landscapes and industries can 
anticipate, absorb, and adapt to drought conditions while maintaining wellbeing, economic 
stability, and environmental sustainability.
Defining drought and drought 
resilience:
Image courtesy: Shire of Narembeen

3.  Defining drought and drought resilience for the Wheatbelt
68
Introduction
Drought is defined 
as a prolonged, 
abnormal dry period 
when the amount 
of water available 
is insufficient to 
meet normal use 
(Australian Bureau of 
Meteorology). 
The need for a Wheatbelt-appropriate definition of drought was raised several times during stakeholder 
consultation for the pilot Southern Wheatbelt Regional Drought Resilience Planning program. Farmers and 
agribusiness deemed ‘growing season rainfall’ rather than ‘annual rainfall’ the more important measure 
of drought in areas dependent on agriculture — with two or more consecutive dry growing seasons 
having severe business and personal consequences. In addition, the growing prevalence of ‘hot droughts’ 
(overlapping periods of low rainfall and high temperatures) was identified as a serious threat to Wheatbelt 
farming operations and communities.
So-called ‘hot droughts’ are defined by total rainfall (mm) during the April to October growing season being 
in decile 1 (the lowest 10% of years on the historical record) and daily maximum air temperatures during the 
August to November grain fill period being in decile 9 (the hottest 10% of years on the historical record).  
Hot droughts can be devastating for the environment and societies, and are expected to increase globally.1,2  
In WA, hot droughts have primarily occurred in the past 15 years (2005–2020) mostly in the coastal regions of 
the southwest but the Wheatbelt has also experienced two hot droughts between 2000 to 2020.3
No definition can adequately measure drought for all people, disciplines, 
locations and timeframes, and drought definitions should be developed and 
applied for a specified context.3
1. 	 Overpeck, J. T. The challenge of hot drought. Nature 503, 350–351 (2013)
2. 	 Bourne, A. R., et al. Hot droughts compromise interannual survival across all group sizes in a cooperatively 
breeding bird. Ecology Letters 23, 1776–1788 (2020)
3. 	 Mastrantonis, S., (2022) Defining drought in Western Australia. Centre for Crop and Disease Management. 
Curtin University, WA.

3.  Defining drought and drought resilience for the Wheatbelt
69
While these meteorological definitions of drought help capture the impact 
of rainfall deficiency and extreme temperatures on broadacre agricultural 
enterprises in the Wheatbelt, a more comprehensive drought definition 
is needed to encapsulate the impacts of drought across Wheatbelt 
communities, landscapes and economies. Technical work commissioned on 
behalf of the federal regional drought resilience process in southern WA 
along with stakeholder consultation across the Wheatbelt developed the 
following drought definition for the Wheatbelt:
‘A prolonged period of abnormally dry conditions 
that impacts negatively on water availability and 
agricultural production in a region and, consequently, 
impacts negatively on the economy and environment 
of the region and the health and wellbeing of its 
residents’
The following meteorological definitions of drought developed 
during the pilot Southern Wheatbelt RDRP focused on total rainfall 
during the April to October growing season:
•	 Drought Year: total rainfall (mm) during the April to October 
growing season is in decile 1 (the lowest 10% of years on the 
historical record)
•	 Severe Drought: total rainfall (mm) during the April to October 
growing season is in decile 1 for two or more consecutive years.
•	 Hot Drought: total rainfall (mm) during the April to October 
growing season is in decile 1 and daily maximum air 
temperatures during the August to November maturing season 
are in decile 9 (the hottest 10% of years on the historical record).

70
Drought policy
Drought policy has 
evolved considerably 
in WA since the 1980s, 
with a policy shift 
towards preparing for 
drought rather than 
distribution of relief 
funding in response to a 
drought declaration (the 
last of which occurred in 
1982 in WA). 
Following successive dry seasons in WA’s eastern and central grainbelt between 2006–2010, WA 
2010–2012, aimed at ensuring farm business preparedness for drought through business management 
training, household and community support and exit grants. Following the success of the pilot reform 
measures, farm business capacity workshops were delivered through the Plan, Prepare and Prosper 
program from 2012–2014. Figure 1 illustrates the history of drought policy in Australia.
The new National Drought Agreement (2024–2029) recognises that drought 
impacts beyond the farm gate and into the broader rural community.

3.  Defining drought and drought resilience for the Wheatbelt
73
The 2018 Drought Agreement was a five-year intergovernmental agreement 
between the Australian, state, and territory governments, and was designed 
to help Australian agriculture prepare for, manage, and recover from drought. 
It established a framework for national coordination of drought policies and 
programs, clarifying the roles and responsibilities of each jurisdiction to 
ensure consistency and reduce duplication. The current agreement runs from 
2024 to 2029 and builds on this first agreement by recognising:
•	
that drought impacts beyond the farm gate and into the broader rural 
community.
•	
that drought policies and programs should cover mental health, social 
cohesion and safety, and that these are issues that can be exacerbated  
by drought.
•	
the need to partner with First Nations peoples, communities and/or 
peak organisations when developing and delivering drought policies and 
programs. 
•	
that the development and delivery of drought policies and programs 
should be evidenced based. 
•	
that there will be times when even the most prepared and resilient may 
need support and that this support should be provided to avoid market 
distortions and that eligibility should be based on need, not activated by 
drought declarations. 
“
“National and State drought policy has shifted 
from crisis management and financial assistance to 
preparedness and risk management.”
Intergovernmental Agreement of National Drought Program 
Reform Working Group

74
3.  Defining drought and drought resilience for the Wheatbelt
“
Drought resilience
Drought resilience is a 
subset of regional and 
community resilience 
and involves an 
interconnected complex 
of observable and 
unobservable factors. 
No one approach can be 
used to build resilience 
across the Wheatbelt. 
Over the course of developing the Wheatbelt RDRP it became clear that a multi-pronged and tailored 
approach was needed to instil long-lasting resilience across Wheatbelt communities and the region’s main 
economy — agriculture.
To capture the complexity and breadth involved in drought resilience in the Wheatbelt the following 
definition was developed through the Wheatbelt RDRP stakeholder consultation process:
“A drought resilient Wheatbelt is one in which communities,  
landscapes and industries can anticipate, absorb, and adapt to  
drought conditions while maintaining wellbeing, economic  
stability, and environmental sustainability.”
“
“When droughts inevitably occur, communities need to be ready — and in 
a way that works for each particular community. With natural disasters, 
including drought, there is no cookie-cutter solution.”
Natalie Egleton, CEO Foundation for Rural & Regional Renewal
74

“
“
All stakeholders consulted through the Wheatbelt RDRP process were asked 
to describe what a resilient, adaptable Wheatbelt looked like. The need for 
collaboration, planning and monitoring was a common thread across all 
responses. The two quotes below come from a Wheatbelt tourism director 
and a regional Telstra communications manager:
“A resilient Wheatbelt embraces innovation, diversity, and 
collaboration, enabling communities and industries to 
withstand external pressures while continuing to grow.”
Linda Vernon, Tourism Officer Wheatbelt Way 
“A resilient Wheatbelt region is prepared for drought 
conditions and has short-, mid- and long-term strategies 
developed to monitor and model conditions and identify 
and act on areas most at risk.”
Boyd Brown, Regional Manager Telstra

4.  Wheatbelt drought vulnerability assessment

4.  Wheatbelt drought vulnerability assessment 
79
• 	 The Wheatbelt as a whole is currently rated moderately vulnerable to the impacts of 
drought with ratings across sub-regions ranging from ‘low-to-moderate’ in the high-rainfall 
west through to ‘very high’ in low rainfall eastern areas
• 	 While the drought vulnerability assessment picks up current and future climatic and 
biophysical threats well, it falls short in properly assessing social, natural and human capital 
and infrastructure status at local (LGA) and sub-regional levels
• 	 Community and regional resilience relies on a suite of factors with some only captured 
through qualitative analysis using case studies and storytelling to unravel the drivers of 
successful resilience
• 	 Addressing these data/status deficiencies will provide depth to future drought vulnerability 
analyses and enable the impact of implemented RDRP projects to be properly assessed

4.  Wheatbelt drought vulnerability assessment 
Introduction
Building drought 
resilience across the 
Wheatbelt requires an 
understanding of where 
the region’s people, 
processes and systems 
currently stand in the 
face of a drying climate. 
The vulnerability index map in Figure 1 was developed by integrating and weighting a range of national 
and regional economic, environmental and social data related to drought vulnerability and resilience in the 
Wheatbelt. The map and its underlying data sets provide a broad snapshot in time of drought vulnerability 
at a sub-regional level across the Wheatbelt. 
The process used to develop the drought vulnerability indices can be repeated (and refined) over time to 
assess the impact of drought resilience measures instigated via the Wheatbelt regional drought resilience 
implementation plan (see Section 10 Implementing the Plan).

“A drought resilient Wheatbelt is prepared for drought conditions and 
uses monitoring and modelling to identify areas of highest risk.

Sub-regional drought 
vulnerability  
To identify issues 
requiring priority action at 
a sub-regional level, the 
data used in Figure 1 
is displayed in more detail 
in Figures 2a–e. 
Each vulnerability assessment was based on the same 10 indicators (Table 1). Drought vulnerability 
indicators were rated on a sliding scale from 1–5, with 1 being the lowest vulnerability and 5 the 
highest (Table 2).
The Wheatbelt as a whole is currently considered moderately vulnerable to future drought and 
climate change, however the drought vulnerability of individual sub-regions is rated as low-medium 
through to very high (Figure 1).
All sub-regions are now dealing with more drought years than they 
did 40 years ago and all have economies heavily reliant on agriculture. 
Economic diversification and continued investment in agricultural 
resilience will be required as the climate dries and becomes hotter.

Drought vulnerability indicator 
Notes on drought vulnerability measure
Projected change in temperature and rainfall
•	 Change in number of days above 34ºC August to November by 2050
•	 Percentage change in rainfall April to October by 2050 (Climate Services for 
Agriculture)
Change in timing of the autumn break
•	 Percentage difference in time of median seasonal break between 1975-1999 
and 2000-2020 (defined as least 25 mm of rainfall over three consecutive days 
before sowing) (DPIRD and BOM)
Change in drought frequency
•	 Drought is defined as growing season rainfall in the 10th percentile and is 
calculated relative to the average seasonal rainfall over the preceding 40 
years. (DPIRD)
Production risk
•	 Incorporates measures of (1) average regional production for wheat, barley, 
canola, sheep and cattle, (2) proportion of high quality agricultural land, (3) 
wheat yield potential and (4) 20-year average NDVI* (DPIRD)
Trend in groundcover (NDVI*) 
•	 Quantifies number of days at the lowest 10% of NDVI (vegetation cover) 
between 2002 and 2021 (DPIRD)
Percentage employment in agriculture
•	 Proportion of employed people in farming and allied industries (Australian 
Census 2016)
Percentage unemployment
•	 Proportion of people at employable age who are registered as unemployed 
(Australian Census 2016)
Access to infrastructure
•	 Scheme water coverage and proximity to main road infrastructure and 
strategic community water supplies (DPIRD)
Groundwater quality
•	 Groundwater salinity level (fresh to hyper saline) (DPIRD)
Soil capability
•	 Land capability for cropping based on analysis and interpretation of DPIRD’s 
soil-landscape mapping dataset 
*	NDVI: normalised difference vegetation index measures the ‘greenness’ of vegetation and is used in studies of climate change, agriculture, 
and natural disasters.

Drought vulnerability indicator
Drought vulnerability sliding 1–5 scale details   
(1= lowest vulnerability and 5= highest vulnerability)
Projected change in temperature and rainfall
1. 	 No increase in number of hot days, no change or an increase in rainfall
2. 	 Moderate increase in number of hot days (<2.5), moderate decrease in rainfall (<15%)
3. 	 High increase in number of hot days (<2.5 more hot days), moderate decrease in rainfall (<15%)
4. 	 Moderate increase in number of hot days (<2.5), large decrease in rainfall (≥15%)
5. 	High increase in number of hot days (≥2.5 more hot days), large decrease in rainfall (≥15%)  
Change in timing of the autumn break
1. 	 Autumn break occurs earlier than the preceding 40-year median
2. 	 Autumn break occurs 0–2% later than the preceding 40-year median
3. 	 Autumn break occurs  3–5% later than the preceding 40-year median
4 . 	Autumn break occurs 6–8% later than the preceding 40-year median
5. 	Autumn break occurs ≥9% later than the preceding 40-year median
Change in drought frequency
1. 	 Three or four fewer droughts than the preceding 40 years
2. 	 One or two fewer drought than the preceding 40 years
3.	 Equal number of droughts as the preceding 40 years
4. 	 One or two more droughts than the preceding 40 years
5. 	Three or four more droughts than the preceding 40 years
Production risk
1. 	 Very low risk — good production, good soils and high average NDVI*
2. 	 Low risk
3. 	 Moderate risk
4. 	 High risk
5. 	Very high risk — marginal production, poor soils and low average NDVI*  
Trend in groundcover (NDVI*) 
1. 	 NDVI*@10% for 181–200 days between 2002 and 2021
2. 	 NDVI @10% for 161–180 days between 2002 and 2021
3. 	 NDVI @10% for 141–160 days between 2002 and 2021
4. 	 NDVI @10% for 121–140 days between 2002 and 2021
5. 	NDVI @10% for 100–120 days between 2002 and 2021
*	NDVI: normalised difference vegetation index measures the ‘greenness’ of vegetation and is used in studies of climate change, agriculture, and natural disasters.

4.  Wheatbelt drought vulnerability assessment 
Measuring and monitoring 
drought resilience 
Significant gaps exist in 
our ability to quantify the 
environmental and social 
impacts of drought and 
capture a true measure of 
adaptive capacity across 
the Wheatbelt. 
For example, the last environmental score card for Western Australia was done nearly 20 years ago in 
2007. The Regional Natural Resource Management Strategy for the (Wheatbelt’s) Avon River Basin was 
released more than a decade ago in 2013 — and the strategy’s main recommendations are still relevant 
and largely unmet — a situation that underscores the investment and coordination needed to enhance 
and protect the Wheatbelt’s unique and dispersed natural capital and implement monitoring systems to 
assess impact of restoration projects..
In addition, very little is known about the true adaptive capacity and 
drought vulnerability of Wheatbelt communities in the face of a drying 
climate. Audits of infrastructure needs and the drought resilience of 
recreational and other green spaces is needed across the Wheatbelt to 
create baseline data sets that can be used in ongoing resilience monitoring.

9.  Wheatbelt drought vulnerability assessment

4.  Wheatbelt drought vulnerability assessment
89
• 	Drought vulnerability is a function of the sensitivity and exposure of people 
and systems to the environmental, social and economic impacts of drought 
• 	Drought resilience is a function of the adaptive capacity of people or systems 
to the environmental, social and economic impacts of drought (Figure 3)
FIGURE 3  Factors contributing to drought vulnerability/resilience of people, processes and systems in the Wheatbelt.  
Source: Department of Primary Industries and Regional Development

90
There is also an urgent need to develop capacity at the LGA level to ensure 
local communities are working collaboratively and strategically to attract 
and manage the infrastructure and services needed to build resilience and 
economic diversity across the Wheabelt in the face of a drier, hotter climate.
A community is considered resilient when ‘members of 
its population are connected to one another and work 
together, so that they are able to function and sustain 
critical systems, even under stress; adapt to changes in 
the physical, social or economic environment; be self-
reliant if external resources are limited or cut off; and 
learn from experience to improve itself over time’1.
Many inspirational examples of the Arbon et al (2016)1 community resilience 
definition can be found across the Wheatbelt’s 200+ communities and 42 local 
government authorities (LGAs) — from individuals banding together to buy local 
grocery shops and pubs on behalf of their towns through to local government 
alliances that build agency and capacity, low-rainfall farmers increasing water 
use efficiency and business strength despite drops in season rainfall, innovative 
aged care and childcare systems developed using local expertise; locally-
developed wireless telecommunication systems; primary industries based on 
First Nation’s cultural and environmental values; and the many thousands of 
committed, long-term volunteers underpinning the region’s sporting,  
fire-fighting, ambulance and social clubs.

5. Wheatbelt water demand
Introduction
The water supply 
and demand 
equation for the 
Wheatbelt is 
complex.
Developing additional water supplies for the Wheatbelt while improving the 
way water is used and prioritised at a sub-regional and local government 
level lie at the heart of solving the water challenges faced across the region. 
Wheatbelt water demand comes from 77,000 residents, 3000+ farms and thousands of commercial 
businesses and public institutions across 15 million hectares of land (Figure 1).
Supply is drawn from an equally diverse mix of sources including desalinated seawater processed south 
of Perth, groundwater aquifers, surface water captured in Mundaring Weir in the Perth hills and in Harris 
and Stirling dams near Collie in the southwest, rainwater captured in hundreds of community and many 
thousands of farm dams, rainwater captured from domestic and commercial rooves and, in some towns, 
recycled wastewater and stormwater.
Water underpins the Wheatbelt’s productivity, ecosystems and liveability and growth of its towns. From 
biological productivity and economic viability of its primary industries, through to the health of its natural 
ecosystems and the capacity of its towns and LGAs to create liveable and socially cohesive communities, 
support new industries, diversify economies and grow populations.
Both supply and demand sides of the Wheatbelt water equation are under increasing strain, and addressing 
challenges will be critical to securing drought resilience for the Wheatbelt. Climate change tops the 
challenge list — placing downward pressure and increased cost on the supply side of the water equation 
while also increasing water demand from livestock and cropping systems, public open spaces and 
households (Figure 1).

5. Wheatbelt water demand

98
5. Wheatbelt water demand
Wheatbelt water 
supply 
Climate change has 
and will continue to 
disrupt water supplies in 
the Wheatbelt through 
reduced run-off into 
storage reservoirs 
and farm dams and 
reduced recharge into 
groundwater aquifers. 
Overlaying the impact of climate change on water supplies, is the growing competition for fresh water as 
Wheatbelt populations grow north and east of Perth and the growing competition for groundwater between 
agriculture, mining and renewable energy operations. Ageing pipeline infrastructure adds further cost and 
complexity. Despite these challenges, the Wheatbelt still has opportunities to secure additional water sources 
and use existing supplies more efficiently. 
Wheatbelt water currently comes from three main sources:
1.	
Drinking water piped in from Perth and Collie via 13,000 kms of pipelines in the Goldfields and  
Agricultural Water Supply Scheme (GAWS) and the Great Southern Towns Water Supply Scheme 
(GSTWSS) (see Figure 2)
2.	 Stored rainfall in water reservoirs and community/farm dams and tanks
3.	 Groundwater and surface water abstracted from aquifers and rivers (predominantly in the Central Coast 
sub-region) 
Recycled waste water is used in some communities across the region. Community desalination is operational in 
the Shires of Merredin and Dumbleyung. 
Drinking water 
Most drinking (potable) water for the Wheatbelt is imported from Perth and Collie (Figure 2) where streamflow 
into catchment dams has fallen by 80% over the past 50 years (Figure 3). As these reservoirs can no longer 
reliably meet demand, desalinated seawater is increasingly filling the gap and is projected to be 65% of 
potable water by 2035 (Figure 4). 
However seawater desalination is energy intensive, requiring more than four times more energy than 
groundwater collection, and over 40 times more than dam-sourced water.

Farm and community dams
Reduced run-off over recent decades has affected farm and community  
dams across the Wheatbelt. Many of the 200,000 farm dams now capture 
only 20–30% of the run-off they received 40-years ago, leaving them unable 
to meet on-farm water demands needed during consecutive low rainfall 
years. As most dryland farms across southwest WA depend almost entirely on 
on-farm water supplies, declining and compromised dam storage has driven 
a sharp increase in water carting and ‘water deficiency declarations’ over the 
past five years (Figure 5).

5. Wheatbelt water demand
101
Seawater desalination is four times more energy intensive than 
groundwater collection and over 40 times more energy intensive 
than water sourced from dams. Securing Wheatbelt water supplies 
will also require using available water more efficiently.

Licensed groundwater and surface water
WA is divided into proclaimed and unproclaimed regions of groundwater,  
with most of the Wheatbelt unproclaimed. Water use is regulated in 
proclaimed regions through licensing, which includes major towns and 
settlements. Water usage in unproclaimed areas is much less restrictive.  
Moora, in the Gingin groundwater region, sits on the boundary between 
proclaimed and unproclaimed areas.
FIGURE 5  Number of ‘water deficiency declarations’ across dryland farming areas in southwest WA over the past 30 years.

farm and community water demands. About 20% of water declarations over the past 30 years have occurred in the Wheatbelt.
Data source: Department of Water and Environmental Regulation. 
Significant supplies of fresh groundwater and limited supplies of fresh  
surface water occur from Gingin to Jurien Bay within the Wheatbelt Central 
Coast sub-region and part of the Central Midlands sub-region (Figure 6).  
The Central Coast sub-region relies completely on licensed groundwater and 
surface water allocations for domestic drinking water and water for mining, 
horticulture and renewable energy operations.

In the Gingin groundwater allocation 
plan area, there are 36 proclaimed 
groundwater resources under the 
Rights in Water and Irrigation Act 
2014, with a total allocation (July 
2025) of 174 GL per year. Twenty-one 
of the groundwater resources are 
fully allocated and a further nine 
include water reserved for future 
public water supply. Surface water 
resources in this area have a total 
allocation of 1.3 GL per year and  
are fully allocated. 
FIGURE 6  Boundary of the Gingin (Wheatbelt 
Central Coast sub-region) groundwater allocation 
plan area and some of the monitoring bore 
locations within the plan area (DWER 2024). Moora 
(Wheatbelt Central Midlands sub-region) is located

5. Wheatbelt water demand
Groundwater levels and stream flows in the Gingin area are being impacted 
by reduced rainfall due to climate change. Since 2011, average annual rainfall 
in Gingin is nine per cent lower than the period 1975 to 2010. Seasonal rainfall 
patterns are also changing. Rainfall volumes over the winter wet season 
are lower, with peak rainfall occurring later in the season, and more rainfall 
occurring over the dry season.1
Monitoring bores across the region’s five major aquifers show mixed trends 
over the past 25 years, reflecting differences in rainfall, abstraction and 
aquifer connectivity. The most concerning decline is in the southern portion 
of the Leederville–Parmelia aquifer (beneath Gingin to Dandaragan), where 
groundwater levels have declined by 10 metres since the 1990s due to reduced 
recharge and cumulative cumulative abstraction (Figures 7a and b). The aquifer 
is now fully allocated and licence trading rules were updated in 2024 to further 
protect aquifer levels and the ecosystems they support, such as Gingin Brook.1

5. Wheatbelt water demand
Wheatbelt water 
demand 
Measuring actual water 
use across the Wheatbelt 
is difficult because many 
of the 3000+ farms rely 
on unlicensed ‘self-
supply’ sources such as 
dams, bores and tanks 
— providing 80–100% 
of their water. These 
volumes are not directly 
measured, and can only be 
estimated from Australian 
Bureau of Statistics data 
of livestock numbers and 
cropping area. 
The data in this section comes from a Wheatbelt water use account developed by the Department of 
Water and Environmental Regulation (DWER). It combines licensed (allocated) groundwater and surface 
water data with estimates of non-licensed water use, scheme water (potable) and farm water. DWER 
is working with the Department of Primary Industries and Regional Development (DPIRD) to refine 
estimates of farm water use and storage. 
Please note: the water data can not be separated for the Avon, Central Midlands and Central East  
sub-regions.
Total water use
In 2021–2022, total water use in the Wheatbelt was estimated at 156 GL (Figure 8a). About 10%  
(15 GL) was piped drinking (potable) water supplied through the Water Corporation (Figure 2), while  
90% (130 GL) came from licensed and unlicensed groundwater and surface water. Recycled wastewater 
made up less than 1%. 
The Central Coast sub-region has the largest water demand — 
consuming nearly three quarters of the Wheatbelt total while the Avon, 
Central Midlands and Central East sub-regions collectively use 20% of 
the total, and the Wheatbelt South sub-region uses 7% (Figure 8a).

5. Wheatbelt water demand
107
The Central Coast, home to 20% of the Wheatbelts population, accounted for 
73% of total water use. The Avon, Central Midlands and Central East  
sub-regions together hold nearly 60% of the Wheatbelt population, but use 
just 20% of total water use (Figure 8b).
Irrigation for horticulture is the region’s largest water user — accounting 
for 60% of total water use in 2021–2022, followed by farm ‘households and 
livestock’ at 15% and mining at 13%. Wheatbelt town households accounted 
for about 7% of total water use (Figure 9).

Drinking (potable) water 
Of the 15 GL of drinking (potable) water piped into 
the Wheatbelt via Water Corporation schemes, 
about 45% is used by town households and 
about 35% by farms (households + livestock). 
The remaining 20% is used by industry and 
commercial operations and to water public open 
spaces (Figure 10). About 80% (12 GL) of potable 
water is used within the Avon, Central Midlands 
and Central East sub-regions and about 20% 
(3 GL) in the Wheatbelt South (Figure 10). The 
Central Coast sub-region sources all its potable 
water from allocated groundwater and surface 
water and has its own local water supply schemes. 
Central Coast water service providers currently 
have licenses totalling about 5 GL for public water 
supply (data not shown).
Licensed (allocated) water
Only about half of all (licensed) groundwater 
and surface water allocated to the Wheatbelt 
is used in any year due to the ebbs and flows in 
demands from horticulture, agriculture and mining 
operations. Of the 240 GL of water licensed to 
Wheatbelt licensees in 2021–2022, about 130 GL 
was abstracted and used.

5. Wheatbelt water demand
Sub-regional 
water use
Access to water varies 
across the Wheatbelt 
sub-regions and water 
supply is constrained by 
a range of challenges. 
Securing water supplies across the Wheatbelt requires a tailored approach at the sub-regional  
and LGA level. For example, not all towns and farming properties have access to Water Corporation potable 
water schemes and must be self-sufficient for 80–100% of their water requirements. Other areas have access 
to fresh groundwater supplies (Central Coast) while much of the groundwater in other areas of the Wheatbelt 
is saline and requires desalination (and a viable business case) to be of use. 
Central Coast sub-region
The Central Coast sub-region accounts for nearly three quarters of the total water use of the Wheatbelt with 
horticulture using 75% of the sub-region’s annual 113.5 GL, and mining about 10% (Figure 13).
Threats to Central Coast water supplies include a fast-growing population (15.4% in the past decade), driven 
by the growth of the Perth metropolitan area to the south, increasing groundwater competition from mining, 
renewables and horticultural projects and declining rainfall, groundwater recharge and streamflow. Combined, 
these impacts reduce the volume of water that can be sustainably abstracted for consumptive uses. 
There is a need to carry out strategic planning and water audits for the  
sub-region to better understand current water resources and future needs 
from industry developments, horticulture and housing. 
DWER is currently developing the Gingin groundwater allocation plan (including scientific investigations and 
numerical modelling) in accordance with the Gingin groundwater and surface water allocation plans: 2024 
evaluation statement.3
3.	 Government of Western Australia (2024) Gingin groundwater and surface water allocation plans: 2024 evaluation statement, 
Department of Water and Environmental Regulation, Perth, Western Australia

5. Wheatbelt water demand
113
Avon, Central Midlands and Central east  
sub-regions
The Avon, Central Midlands and Central East sub-regions collectively use  
20% of total Wheatbelt water with ‘farm households + livestock’ using 
about a third of the sub-regions’ annual 31.8 GL followed by horticulture at 
22% (Figure 14). Fresh groundwater is limited across these sub-regions — 
especially in the Avon and Central East. The impact of climate change on the 
volume of runoff into dams represents a threat to water supply alongside 
rising competition for water between mining and renewable energy projects 
and the ongoing maintenance requirements of the 120-year-old potable 
water pipeline (see Figure 2). There is a need to assess farm water storage 
infrastructure across the Avon, Central Midlands and Central East sub-regions 
to ensure it can meet future water demand for stock and spraying across a 
range of climate scenarios.
Wheatbelt South sub-region
The Wheatbelt South sub-region uses just 7% of total Wheatbelt water and 
relies largely on self–supplied water captured in dams and tanks along with 
potable scheme water via the Great Southern Towns Water Supply Scheme 
(GSTWSS) (see Figure 2). 
Of the 10.8 GL of estimated total water use in the Wheatbelt South,  
two thirds is used by farm households and livestock (Figure 15).  
With 80–100% of farm water in the Wheatbelt South needing to be self-
supplied via dams and tanks, there is a need to ensure dams in the sub-region 
are engineered to optimise runoff — especially during below-average rainfall 
seasons. Repeated hot, dry conditions in this sub-region in recent years have 
highlighted the need to plan for multi-year droughts at the sub-regional and 
farm level.

5. Wheatbelt water demand
Water planning
Each Wheatbelt 
sub-region has a 
relatively unique 
pattern of water use, 
and water management 
and planning for 
the Wheatbelt will 
therefore require a 
nuanced approach 
(Figure 16). 
Sub-regional water threats
Figure 17 summarises annual water use across the Wheatbelt by water source and purpose and lists 
possible drought resilience measures that could be implemented to either use water more efficiently or 
store and capture water more effectively. The graph highlights how varied water use is across Wheatbelt 
end users, and underscores the need for a tailored approach to water management across the region. 
The major water threats facing the sub–regions can be summarised as:
• 	 Wheatbelt South: reduced reliability of dams for stock water; limited access to 
scheme (potable) water 
• 	 Central Coast: increasing competition for groundwater due to population growth 
and mining/renewables/horticulture/intensive livestock developments
• 	 Avon, Central Midlands, Central East: reduced reliability of dams, increasing age 
and maintenance needs of scheme water pipeline, competition for groundwater 
(Moora in the Central Midlands), population growth and potable water demand 
(west Avon).

5. Wheatbelt water demand
Building drought 
resilience
Despite growing 
constraints on Wheatbelt 
water supplies, 
opportunities exist 
across the Wheatbelt 
at the town, farm and 
sub-regional level to 
build drought resilience 
by securing alternative 
water supplies and by 
using current water 
supplies more efficiently. 
The following case studies illustrate the water supply and demand issues playing out across the Wheatbelt  
and the solutions being trialled and implemented at the local government level to lift water security and 
drought resilience. 
Shire-level drought resilience
The 200+ towns across the Wheatbelt use about 12% or 19 GL of the region’s total annual water (156 GL). 
Just over half (˜10 GL) is used by households, with 13% (2.5 GL) used to water public open spaces and the 
remainder used by industry/power generation and commercial businesses/public institutions (˜3 GL each) 
(Figure 18). 
The proportion of total Wheatbelt water used to irrigate parks, gardens and recreational amenities is very 
small at just 1.5%, but having green spaces over the summer and especially during dry seasons, has a large 
impact on the social cohesion and liveability of Wheatbelt towns. About 35% of the water used to irrigate 
parks, gardens and recreational spaces in the Wheatbelt is sourced from scheme water piped in via the Water 
Corporation’s water network. Using recycled wastewater reduces local government water costs considerably, 
but not all shires have access to recycled wastewater and, even when they do, irrigation demands during dry 
seasons can outstrip supply.
A common theme arising from the case study interviews is the need to 
empower local governments with the skills and funding capacity to solve 
their specific water issues and ensure sustainable success.

Image courtesy:  Tourism Western Australia

Williams Shire
Sub-Region:  Wheatbelt South
Population (2021):  1040 (40% in town)
Population growth (2016–2021):  3.8% 
Industries:  Agriculture (dryland); industrial precinct; tourism
Annual rainfall:  566 mm
Potable water use (2023):  0.08 GL per year;  
~1.5 million litres/week
Water issues:
“
• 	 Increased capacity needed at LGA-level to carry out strategic 
analysis of water threats and opportunities
• 	 Over-reliance on scheme water with no back-up or redundancy 
if the pipeline fails for any reason
• 	 Significant cost switching to scheme water to irrigate local 
ovals when recycled wastewater runs out
• 	 Competition for potable water from roadworks and windfarm 
development
• 	 Competition for local groundwater from bauxite mining west 
of the shire
When interviewed on 20 May 2025, Williams Shire CEO 
Peter Stubbs forecast that the town would run out of 
recycled wastewater for irrigating the local sports fields  
and parks within two days. 
“Luckily it’s going to rain this weekend, so we might just get through 
without needing to use scheme water this year. Last year we had to 
switch to scheme water in early April and it cost us $20,000. Other 
shires without recycled wastewater systems are paying $50,000 to 
$100,000 or more each year to irrigate their ovals.”
Williams’ scheme (drinking) water is delivered via the Great 
Southern Towns Water Supply Scheme (GSTWSS) from Harris 
and Stirling dams near Collie. The town currently has no back-up 
potable water supply and Peter is concerned they could be left 
without water if the pipeline system was to fail. 
“Three months back a truck ran into the pipeline between Williams 
and Narrogin and ruptured the pipe — which was a major issue. 
That sort of event could see a town like Williams without a water 
supply for days or even weeks — the public toilets would be shut 
with no water to run them and you’d then be faced with potentially 
evacuating the schools and dealing with the impacts on local 
businesses like the roadhouse and cafes who couldn’t operate 
without water.”
122
5. Wheatbelt water demand

“
 “I’d be more comfortable if we had a million litres of drinking water storage 
on top of the hill so if the scheme supply goes down we know we’ve got four 
or five days’ worth of rationed supply.”
The shire would like to expand its water storage capacity and has received 
funding through the Community Water Supplies Partnership program 
(administered by the WA Department of Water and Environmental Regulation) 
to install strategic tanks for firefighting water across the district. 
“There’s a limit to roof catchment but there’s also an option to expand our 
catchment storage by capturing excess water from roads and big roof 
structures like the CBH — and pump it to a storage tank so it can come down  
to town using gravity. We could also develop lined dams  
to capture and store water.” 
Increasing storage requires strategic planning and Peter says small local 
governments like his do not have the in-house expertise to do the analysis.
“If we’re going to increase storage we need consultants or Water Corporation 
engineers to examine what’s possible — there needs to be a strategic 
assessment around the key vulnerabilities of our system and what we can 
practically do about it.”
Peter has been involved in discussions around water through the Voluntary 
Organisation of Councils — a cooperative body made up of local councils 
within a specific region who gather to discuss common issues at a local 
government level.
“The challenges in each town are quite 
different. It’s good that we can get funding 
for infrastructure like water tanks but we 
also need help with some fundamental 
analysis. What are our top five priorities 
and what can we do about them?”
Peter Stubbs, Williams Shire CEO

5. Wheatbelt water demand
Dumbleyung Shire
Sub-Region:  Wheatbelt South
Population (2021):  681
Population growth (2016–2021):  1.5% 
Industries:  Agriculture (dryland); aquaculture (yabbies); tourism
Annual rainfall:  390 mm
Potable water use (2023):  0.06 GL per year;  
~1.1 million litres/week
Water issues:
“
• 	 Increased local capacity needed to operate town 
desalination plant 
• 	 Strategic approach required to solve water issues at scale
• 	 Lack of rain and run-off has adversely affected local parks 
and sporting facilities
• 	 High water table has damaged community infrastructure 
• 	 Pool closure has affected community — socially and 
economically
• 	 Competition for water from road building/maintenance
Dumbleyung Shire CEO Gavin Treasure says a lack of dam 
water to irrigate the main Dumbleyung sports oval over the 
dry 2025 summer has forced the shire to pivot to scheme 
water irrigation at significant cost to the Council’s budget. 
“We received significant feedback from concerned local residents 
over the deteriorated condition of our main ovals to play sport. 
Scheme water is expensive but we are willing to pay as having 
access to a quality sports oval during the winter sports season is 
non-negotiable.” 
The shire is hoping to go some way towards addressing its irrigation 
water issue by desalinating the saline groundwater currently 
rising beneath the town that is causing significant damage to 
infrastructure. 
Gavin says the shire is also trying to improve rainfall capture by 
cleaning out its major dams and assessing the efficiency with which 
surface catchments are capturing water. “But in a drying climate 
we’ve decided that desalination of our groundwater resource is a 
good option to explore.”
Dumbleyung is one of three pilot towns taking part in the 
WaterSmart Farms desalination project being overseen by the 
Department of Primary Industries and Regional Development, the 
WA Water Corporation and Murdoch University. The project is in its 
final year and funding is coming to an end.

5. Wheatbelt water demand
125
“
The desalination plant has so far generated about 4.4 million litres of fresh 
‘permeate’ water and disposed about 6.1 million litres of brine ‘reject’ water 
into a decommissioned dam that has been saline for decades. 
“We have recently doubled our desalination-produced water storage 
capacity to about two million litres of permeate water and we now shandy 
this with scheme water to irrigate the oval. Previous 2015 Strategic Water 
Assessments have estimated that Dumbleyung townsite requires circa  
21 million litres of water per annum to provide a sustainable supply to 
meet recreational irrigation requirements. So whilst desalination supply 
won’t necessarily offset this demand, it certainly provides another valuable 
resource for public use.”
The desalinated permeate water is currently costing about $2.70 per kilolitre 
(excluding internal wage costs) to produce, which compares favourably with 
the cost of scheme water at $3.01 per kilolitre. Operating the new technology 
has not always been simple, and the shire is working to overcome issues with 
help from project partners and desalination plant suppliers. 
“Desalination plants are complex and you need someone with the skillset and 
experience to respond to issues — so there’s a knowledge issue and also a 
finance issue with running a plant — how do we successfully embed this new 
operation into our local government business?”
Gavin believes desalination at scale in Wheatbelt towns is an essential part of 
securing local water supplies.
“We haven’t got a water supply problem, we have a water quality problem — 
we’ve already got large quantities of mostly brackish to saline water beneath the 
surface — and we are now learning how to economically extract and store it.” 
Gavin is hoping the town can use what they have learned from the project and 
plan for a larger desalination plant.
“Across the Wheatbelt we don’t have a strategic outlook in terms of how our 
water supplies are changing — we know the climate is warmer and drier and 
we know it’s impacting agriculture and our towns — but we are responding 
through very project-specific eyes.”
“We need to take a step back and look at things across the board, at a macro 
strategic level, and try and come up with an action plan to understand and 
respond to our water shortfall at scale.”
“The only reason we have got this far is because we’ve 
had support from project people who knew what they 
were doing. If local governments are going to embed 
desalination operations in their towns there’s an ongoing 
requirement to support local governments and build 
capacity confidence and knowledge at the local level.”
Gavin Treasure, Dumbleyung Shire CEO

housing developments are also in the pipeline within other

don’t even have the money I need to maintain the roads we’ve got.

5. Wheatbelt water demand
Creating drought-resilient sub-regions across the 
Wheatbelt will require a detailed understanding of 
water supply and demand issues at a local level. 
Water issues vary considerably across the Wheatbelt’s five  
sub-regions and addressing water threats and opportunities 
will therefore require a range of approaches. 
For example, population in the Central Coast sub-region has 
grown 15.4% since 2015 and housing, horticulture, mining 
and, increasingly, renewable energy developments are now 
competing for finite allocations of fresh groundwater. 
By contrast, hundreds of kilometres away in the Central East, 
where population has fallen by 4% since 2015, farmers are 
investigating ways to viably tap into and desalinate brackish 
to saline groundwater to secure livestock water during  
dry seasons. 
Sub-regional drought resilience

“

Former Moora Shire President Tracy Lefroy believes
