“
“There are plans to construct 400 or 500 turbines in our area and they will 
need something like 500 kilolitres for every footing that is built — on top of 
that is water demand for dust suppression. There is a lack of planning and 
definitely a lack of consultation and engagement with local government 
about these water requirements.” 
Tracy says the shire came within five days of running out of water for green 
space irrigation a couple of years ago and increased their wastewater storage 
capacity to better meet demand. “We are looking at the potential of further 
extending our sewerage plant to see if some of that water can be treated and 
used in cement manufacturing for the footings. We will probably also need 
some form of desalination and if we do that we will need to double or perhaps 
treble our water storage capacity.”
The shire currently has the capacity to produce about a gigalitre of recycled 
wastewater each year which is used to irrigate green spaces and for light 
industrial needs. “But we will need to increase this substantially to cover the 
water demand from the wind turbine footings and we will need to produce 
water of specific quality for the cement too.”
“Our aquifer is slow recharging due to clay in the area — it’s under a fair 
amount of pressure already and with 300 to 400 workers coming to town to 
build the turbines — it’s going to put more pressure on the aquifer.” 
“Even with initiatives like stormwater injection it’s likely to take 50 years 
before that injected water has an impact on aquifer recharge.” 
“Mining development will also have an impact — we have mining companies 
drawing water out of the aquifer along with a substantial commercial feedlot 
and a commercial piggery and I don’t really think we know how much water 
is being drawn and we’ve got no sense of future water demand and whether 
there will be anything left over.”
Tracy says Moora’s neighbouring shire, Dandaragan (Central Coast sub-
region), grew by 4.5% in population in 2023–2024 despite initial projections 
expecting a growth of just 0.4%. “We service a lot of Dandaragan’s needs so 
this growth could put more pressure on our water resources.”
“We need to do a lot more planning and study into future demands and how 
we will cope.”
“We really want to be prepared because we are getting very close to these 
renewable projects commencing and we run the risk of being too late in our 
water planning process — how do we plan rapidly but still be accurate?”

5. Wheatbelt water demand
Securing alternative 
water supplies for the 
Wheatbelt
Outputs from the new 
climate model known as 
CMIP6 (Coupled Model 
Intercomparison Project, 
Phase 6) indicate there is 
a 90% chance of a further 
20–30% rainfall decline 
over southwest WA (and 
therefore the Wheatbelt) 
in the next few decades 
(Figure 19). 
CMIP6 is a new generation climate model that gathers climate models from research groups around the 
world to simulate past and future climate scenarios. The models simulate the physics, chemistry and biology 
of the atmosphere, land and oceans in great detail, and require some of the largest supercomputers in the 
world to generate the CMIP6 climate projections.
Australian climate researchers have used the CMIP6 to forecast the climate outlook for Australia out  
to 2080–2099 compared with the period 1995–2014 under a high emissions (RCP8.5) scenario.4 Of the  
33 models run as part of the forecasting exercise 90% predict a significant drying of southwest Australia  
(and therefore the Wheatbelt) between June and November (Figure 19). 
Growing season rainfall has already dropped by as much as 50–60 mm or 20% in 
some areas of the Wheatbelt over the past 90 years — with concomitant impacts 
on community and agricultural water supplies. CMIP6 projections of further 
rainfall reductions underline the importance of securing alternative water 
supplies for the Wheatbelt — especially for periods of successive dry seasons — 
and of capturing and safely storing more rainfall during good seasons.
4.	 Grose, M. R., et al. (2020). Insights from CMIP6 for Australia’s future climate. Earth’s Future, 8

5. Wheatbelt water demand
133
FIGURE 19  CMIP6 rainfall projection (% decline) for June-Nov for 1995–2014 to 2080–2099 under  
high emissions (RCP8.5). Dark brown stippling indicates >90% model agreement on sign of change, 
while blue triangles show regions of small change.  Note: The Wheatbelt is within the dark brown stippling. 
Source: Grosse et al (2020)5
Wheatbelt groundwater exists in compartments and 
not in one easy-to-access aquifer and the challenge 
is locating water sources of sufficient quantity 
and quality. Paleo-channels (ancient underground 
river systems) hold the greatest promise for water 
extraction and desalination at a scale suitable for 
community or industry water demand. Fractured rock 
aquifers are typically localised and have potential as 
sources of groundwater for on-farm water supply.
Regardless of the type of groundwater, most 
Wheatbelt groundwater is brackish-to-saline (apart 
from the freshwater aquifers in the Central Coast 
sub-region) (see Figure 20a and b) and will require 
desalination for most farm and community uses. The 
challenge is to develop abstraction and treatment 
systems that are economically viable, and which can 
out-compete the cost of accessing emergency water 
($8–9/kL) and ultimately scheme water ($2.66/kL for 
domestic customers; higher for major customers). 
If this cost barrier could be overcome, desalination 
could help supplement or even secure water supplies 
in some parts of the Wheatbelt. 
The Water Smart Farms R&D project is improving 
access to groundwater for farms and regional 
communities and assessing the feasibility of farm-
scale desalination units. As part of the project, 
evaluation of 30 early adopters of farm desalination 
systems is being carried out across the Wheatbelt 
and a pilot project is nearing completion to 
investigate community-scale desalination units in 
several Wheatbelt towns — including Dumbleyung, 
Katanning and Merredin.

5. Wheatbelt water demand
135
Salt levels are measured in milligrams per litre.
• 	 Fresh:  	
Less than 500 mg of salt per litre 
• 	 Marginal: 	
Between 500–1,000 mg of salt per litre
• 	 Brackish: 	
Between 1,000–5,000 mg of salt per litre
• 	 Saline: 	
Between 5,000–35,000 mg of salt per litre
• 	 Hyper-saline: 	More than 35,000 mg of salt per litre
Farm desalination plants
Early adopters of on-farm desalination occur mostly in the southern and 
south-eastern areas of the Wheatbelt where the scheme pipeline network is 
less prevalent (Figure 21). Many of the 50 desalination units were installed on 
farms in response to the Decile 1–2 seasons of 2018–2020 (R George pers. 
comm. 2025).
Most of the 50 units were matched to existing farm bores whose reliability 
was not known, and issues with bore flow rates may have contributed to 
performance issues with some desalination units. New production bores 
drilled as part of the Water Smart Farms project have since resulted in 
successful outcomes for many project participants, with particular success 
in the development of fractured rock exploration techniques.  Yields of up to 
100kL/day were derived from many of the successful bores.
All early adopters indicated securing livestock water 
during summer was the main driver of installing 
desalination units — but many now use the water for  
crop spraying as it is of such high quality. 
Most of the early adopters have access to standpipe (scheme) water but the 
time (and cost) involved in lining up to cart the water were major incentives for 
exploring on-farm water from desalination. 
Early results from the on-farm desalination operations indicate that the 
operating costs of desalination are higher than the supply charge for scheme 
water ($2.66/kL for domestic/small-scale customers), and considerably more 
expensive than dam or suitable quality bore water. However, the combined 
capital and operating desalination costs are likely less than, or similar to, 
the costs of carting water ($8–9/kL) while also being more convenient and 
offering superior quality water, especially for crop spraying. Total capital 
costs of a farm-scale desalination unit start at around $50–100,000. Solar 
powered units have a higher capital cost but a lower cost per kilolitre of  
water produced.
Image courtesy:  Ben White

137
Community desalination plants
Affordable water for irrigation of public sporting and recreational amenities 
is critical to maintaining green spaces and social cohesion within regional 
communities. 
Many towns across the Wheatbelt do not have access  
to recycled wastewater for irrigation and must use 
scheme water or let their amenities die over the 
summer months and during dry seasons. Some local 
governments are absorbing $50–100,000 per year to 
water green spaces with scheme water. 
Wheatbelt shires are working with the Water Corporation, Murdoch University 
and the Department of Primary Industries and Regional Development 
(as part of the Water Smart R&D program) to investigate the potential of 
desalinating groundwater to lower rising water tables beneath their towns 
while generating a source of irrigation water for community amenities and 
firefighting purposes. The project is in its second and final year.
The proof-of-concept community desalination plants established at 
Dumbleyung, Katanning and Merredin are in the early stages of being 
assessed but all have demonstrated that desalinated groundwater can be a 
cost-effective source of irrigation water for public open spaces. The challenge 
now is to develop viable ways to scale up the desalination units to replace 
scheme water while ensuring local shires are supported as they embed 
desalination technical capacity in their day-to-day operations.

Sub-Region:  Wheatbelt South

“
Dumbleyung Shire desalination project manager Dave 
Williams says many Wheatbelt towns do not have a good 
sitting underneath them nor the true cost of scheme water 
supply, which is highly subsidised. 
“This desalination program could be a game changer for small 
communities and for the state because it provides the local 
community with a reliable, cost-effective water supply for 
firefighting, for their swimming pools, for their ovals and gardens.”
Dave believes the project has been a catalyst for the community to

resources.

future. It’s not a silver bullet, but it does provide us with a stop gap.”
The Shire of Dumbleyung has also been successful in securing 
further State Government funding through the Community Water

main dams, establish a new concrete silt trap to its main town dam

this dam.

Merredin Shire
Sub-Region:  Central East

A trial community desalination plant in Merredin is helping 
lower the saline water table under the town, which has been 
compromising building foundations and brickwork in local 
buildings. Shire of Merredin CEO, Craig Watts says the goal 
is to use the desalinated water to supplement scheme water 
irrigation of green spaces across the town.
“The cost of desalinated water is now becoming comparable to 
the cost of scheme water, and even the maintenance costs of 
the system are outweighed by the benefits of having easy and 
sustainable access to an alternative water source while lowering the 
groundwater table under the town.”
With the desalination project coming to an end, the Merredin shire is 
hoping to extend the trial for another couple of years to investigate 
a new reverse-osmosis filtering system and maintain access to 
Water Corporation expertise for a bit longer to train staff and build 
local knowledge around desalination.
“We want to establish a specialist training program for our local 
government staff that covers all aspects of non-potable water 
capture, storage, treatment and irrigation to ensure we’re getting 
the most efficient use of all of our non-potable water.”
Craig says local governments tend to have high staff turnover, and 
documenting processes and training staff as they come on board 
will underpin the success of the desalination plant — a complex 
piece of equipment that needs specialist expertise to run.

Recycled wastewater
Across the Wheatbelt only a small amount of recycled wastewater is 
generated — but where it is produced, it is highly valued as an alternative 
to scheme water use on public amenities. 
5. Wheatbelt water demand
Just half of the 42 Wheatbelt LGAs have operating wastewater recycling 
systems and, of these, eight are not generating any recycled water  
(Figure 22). The wastewater treatment systems operate at a range of 
efficiencies — with some reclaiming 70–100% of wastewater generated by 
the town and others less than 50% (Figure 22).

~ 38.6 million litres/week
Recycled wastewater production:  ~1 GL per year “
Northam is the largest regional centre of the Wheatbelt 
and was one of the first local governments to establish a 
recycled wastewater system — about 40 years ago. The 
system currently generates about one gigalitre of irrigation 
water for the town (Figure 24).
Shire of Northam executive manager of development and 
community services, Chadd Hunt, says upgrading the ageing 
wastewater infrastructure is a major focus for the town.
“The Water Corporation is undertaking a major upgrade of their 
wastewater treatment plant and facilities, which will provide the 
shire with more recycled water and for longer throughout the year 
but our current wastewater distribution and storage infrastructure 
will not be capable of accepting the increasing supply.”
If the additional recycled water cannot be used it will be sent down 
the Avon River that runs through the Northam townsite.
“The recycled water irrigates our two main ovals and our main parks 
and reserves in town, and it is used by the high school, the Harness 
Racing Club and the Turf Club, so it is a significant community 
asset.”
Chadd sees increasing wastewater storage capacity as critical given 
the challenges faced by a drying climate, and longer summers. 
144
5. Wheatbelt water demand
Image courtesy:  Shire of Northam

5. Wheatbelt water demand
145
“In some dry seasons we have to supplement the 
recycled water with scheme water at a significant cost.”
Chad Hunt, Northam Shire Executive Manager
An engineering consultancy commissioned by the shire has identified a need 
for significant investment to upgrade the wastewater distribution and storage 
facilities. 
Northam Shire CEO Debbie Terelink says it will cost about $10 million to 
replace the wastewater distribution infrastructure and potentially expand the 
town’s storage capacity. “We don’t have the funding to do that so have been 
advocating quite hard with the State Government because if we can’t take 
the water, it will end up down the river and we will need to use the scheme to 
water our assets.”
Chadd says the local Turf Club supplements its wastewater use with 
harvested stormwater. “A fairly major upgrade was done about ten years ago 
through Royalties for Regions where they constructed a large storage dam 
and a pipeline to feed the dam.”
In winter months when there is no demand for the reuse water town, it is 
pumped from the Water Corporation treatment plant out to the racecourse 
and stored in the large dam use for use in summer, rather than being 
discharged to the river.
“But even with that extra dam storage, there is still some discharge into 
the river in the winter so the challenge for us is storage and this will be 
exacerbated once the Water Corporation boost capacity of the wastewater 
plant in the next few years.”

5. Wheatbelt water demand

5. Wheatbelt water demand

148
5. Wheatbelt water demand
Capturing and storing 
more water in the 
Wheatbelt
To help mitigate the 
decline in annual rainfall 
and increase in drought 
months predicted for 
the Wheatbelt over the 
next few decades, a 
widespread and strategic 
approach to water 
harvesting and storage, 
both on-farm and in 
communities, will 
be critical.
Farm water
‘WaterSmart Dams — making dams work again’ was initiated by the Grower Group Alliance in WA in 2022 to 
develop improved technology and water planning tools to ensure farm dams can capture, harvest and store 
enough water to meet industry needs in a drying climate. Until relatively recently, the Wheatbelt’s 200,000 
private farm dams reliably serviced the water needs of the region’s agricultural enterprises. However, 
prolonged dry conditions over the three years to 2021 resulted in many farm dams failing to capture enough 
water which, in turn, resulted in a record 12 water deficiency declarations across the region and the carting of 
$3.3 million emergency water supplies for livestock.
The project involves 12 demonstration sites, building farm-based water planning tools, workshops, field days 
and industry training. Activities include renovating existing dams, building new dams, and implementing 
evaporation suppression and runoff technologies.
Community water
A network of strategic community water supplies — dams, tanks, bores and other assets — have been 
developed across WA’s dryland agricultural areas to provide a source of emergency non-potable water for 
livestock and firefighting needs during times of low rainfall (Figure 23). Vesting of the emergency water 
supplies and assets varies, with some sites owned by the local government authority, the Water Corporation, 
or by private entities where an agreement has been made to allow access.

5. Wheatbelt water demand
149
A rebuilt ‘roaded catchment’, dam 
and silt trap at Kukerin in the  
south-east Wheatbelt (between 
Dumbleyung and Lake Grace). 
The compacted surface of roaded 
catchment results in water run-
off on about 8–12mm of rainfall 
compared to the 25mm required 
with older dam technology. Lining 
catchments with recycled CBH 
tarps enables run-off following 
rainfall events of just 0.8mm. 
Photo: Peter Clifton, Grower Group Alliance.
Image courtesy: Shire of Kondinin

150
5. Wheatbelt water demand
Strategic community dams are a subset of the so-called ‘agricultural area’ 
(AA) dams constructed during the late 1920s and 1940s as part of the soldier 
settlement agricultural expansion after WW1 and WW2. The network was 
extended in the 1960s and 1970s in response to severe droughts. There are 
about 480 of the original 681 AA dams that range from high value to no value 
in terms of their condition and serviceability. An AA dam is classified as 
strategic if (1) it contains water of acceptable quality for livestock use and a 
capacity, depth and catchment which will maximise the availability of water 
in low rainfall years, and (2) if it forms part of a network within which most 
farmers not connected to a Water Corporation piped scheme can obtain water 
at a distance of less than 40 kilometres. The WA Department of Water and 
Environmental Regulation (DWER) uses LGA maps to determine which sites 
are worth upgrading and to identify priority areas to develop new strategic 
community water supplies.
Strategic community water supplies have received significant funding and 
upgrades over the past 25 years through the Community Water Supplies 
Partnership program (administered by DWER) (see Figure 25 and Table 1). 
The program provides grants of up to $100,000 for community water supply 
improvements in dryland agricultural areas receiving less than 600 mm 
average annual rainfall. The aim of the program is to encourage the planning 
and establishment of reliable non-potable water supplies to meet emergency 
farmland and firefighting water needs and reduce scheme water use. 
Since 2000, the Wheatbelt has received about  
$9.4 million in funding across 38 of its 42 LGAs  
(Figure 26) with the Wheatbelt South sub-region 
receiving the bulk of the funding (Figure 27).

6. Primary industries — overview
163
• 	 Agriculture underpins the economy of the Wheatbelt contributing more than 25% of the 
region’s annual gross value added (GVA) 
• 	 Agriculture is the largest employer across all Wheatbelt sub-regions and about 45% of all 
Wheatbelt businesses are connected with the agricultural industry 
• 	 Broadacre cropping and livestock account for about 80% of the Wheatbelt’s annual 
agricultural production value and rely totally on seasonal rainfall for their success 
• 	 Horticulture and intensive livestock production make up the remaining 20% of the Wheatbelt’s 
annual agricultural production value and rely almost entirely on fresh groundwater supplies in 
aquifers under the Wheatbelt’s Central Coast sub-region

6. Primary industries — overview
Introduction
As the name suggests, 
the Wheatbelt was 
built on agriculture, 
and while the region’s 
economy has since 
diversified, agriculture 
remains a mainstay of 
the economy — with 
$4.4 billion in total 
production value 
generated in 2021. 
This section details the relative size and value of the primary industries across the Wheatbelt and highlights 
their dependence on seasonal rainfall and fresh groundwater supplies.
Broadacre dryland cropping and hay production is the largest agricultural 
industry — generating 75% of the region’s total agricultural production 
value. Livestock for meat (lamb, beef, chicken and pork) and products 
(wool, eggs) account for about a fifth of the annual production value and 
horticulture for the remaining five percent (Figure 1). 
The Wheatbelt’s primary industries are underpinned by:
•	 A large export-oriented broadacre grains industry dominated by wheat, barley 
and canola
•	 A broadacre livestock industry dominated by about six million sheep and about 
150,000 cattle 
•	 Rising poultry (>800,000) and pig numbers (190,000)
•	 A significant and growing horticultural sector with oranges, carrots, lettuce and 
broccoli representing the most valuable crops

6. Primary industries — overview
Wheatbelt — 
broadacre grains 
Broadacre grain and sheep 
dominate the Avon, Central 
Midlands and Central East 
sub-regions while the 
Central Coast sub-region is 
responsible for most of the 
horticultural and intensive 
livestock (pork, poultry) 
produced in the Wheatbelt.
About half of the Wheatbelt’s annual grain production value comes from wheat (51%) followed by feed 
and malt barley (25%) canola (14%), oats (5%) and lupin (4%). Legume pulse crops (field peas, faba 
beans and chickpeas) make up a small but growing sector of the broadacre grains industry (Figure 2). 
Broadacre grains are produced under rainfed conditions and annual yields 
depend solely on the amount and timing of seasonal rainfall.

livestock industries

account for about 20%

of the region’s sheep and cattle industry rely almost exclusively on rain-fed farm dams for water supply.
WA manages about 20% (˜12 million) of the national sheep flock and the 
Wheatbelt has the highest sheep density in WA — about 6 million sheep.

increasingly important as the climate dries.

7. Primary industries — building drought resilience 
Introduction
Water use efficiency 
lies at the heart of 
successful primary 
industries in the 
Wheatbelt.
This section outlines the characteristics of successful primary industries and the action needed to lift 
drought resilience across Wheatbelt horticultural and broadacre enterprises.
Drought resilient primary industries are underpinned by:
•	 High water use efficiency
•	 Best practice agronomy and livestock management
•	 High equity
•	 Strong financial and risk management 
•	 Economies of scale (broadacre agriculture, horticulture and livestock)
•	 High productivity per unit of arable land (horticulture enterprises)
“
“A drought resilient Wheatbelt is one where farm businesses have the 
financial capacity to invest in their businesses and their communities, 
year on year.”
Wheatbelt RDRP consultancy feedback

7. Primary industries — building drought resilience

7. Primary industries — building drought resilience

7. Primary industries — building drought resilience 
184
Building drought 
resilience — horticulture 
Significant opportunity 
exists for improved 
water use efficiency in 
Wheatbelt horticultural 
enterprises.
The Central Coast sub-region accounts for nearly three quarters of the Wheatbelt’s annual water use 
with horticulture using 75% of the sub-region’s annual 113.5 GL (see Section 5 Wheatbelt Water Demand). 
The Leederville–Parmelia aquifer running underneath the Wheatbelt horticultural areas of Gingin to 
Dandaragan is now fully allocated and licence trading rules were updated in 2024 to further protect aquifer 
levels and the groundwater dependent ecosystems they support.1
A fast-growing Central Coast population (15.4% in the past decade) 
and increasing groundwater competition from mining and renewables 
developments coupled with declining rainfall, groundwater recharge and 
streamflow, underpins the need for strategic planning and water audits in this 
sub-region to better understand current and future water needs from industry 
developments, horticulture, and housing.
1.	 Government of Western Australia (2024) Gingin groundwater and surface water allocation 
plans: 2024 evaluation statement, Department of Water and Environmental Regulation, 
Perth, Western Australia

185
185
7. Primary industries — building drought resilience 
Along with water use, there is also considerable scope to lift drought 
resilience through improvements in productivity across Wheatbelt 
horticultural businesses. The 2023 Planfarm WA horticultural benchmarks 
(to which growers in the Wheatbelt contributed) indicate that horticultural 
businesses across the state are facing an unsustainable operating 
environment and are currently using a high risk business model.
Monitoring and improving water use efficiency and 
building business and production capacity within the 
horticultural industry will become increasingly important 
as the climate dries and water competition between 
industries and peri-urban population growth increases.
Businesses in the highest performing benchmarking cohort  
were characterised by:
•	 Productivity: conversion of a high proportion of arable land 
to income — with this metric outweighing crop type, scale and 
location. 
•	 Strong supply agreements: strong relationships and contracts 
with buyers outweighed crop type, scale and location.
The 2023 Planfarm data indicated that:
•	 operating expenses of the average business had increased by 
nearly a third (28%) between 2019–2023
•	 operating profits had declined from 22% to 16% since 2019 — 
due predominantly to inflationary pressures, high interest rates 
and increasing labour costs
•	 return on capital dropped by half — from 6.3% to 3% (with this 
ratio being impacted by rising land asset values)

7. Primary industries — building drought resilience 
1.	 Kingwell, R, Anderton, L, Islam, N, Xayavong, V, Wardell-Johnson, A, Feldman, D & Speijers, J 2013, Broadacre farmers adapting to 
a changing climate, National Climate Change Adaptation Research Facility, Gold Coast, 171 pp.
186
Building drought resilience 
— broadacre industries 
Despite a 20% drop in 
rainfall across south-west 
WA since the 1970s grain 
yields have continued to 
increase (Figure 1). 
Improved genetics, agronomy, machinery and economies of scale mean growers now achieve returns from 
seasons that would have been loss makers decades ago. However, yield increases in the 2000s have been 
smaller and more variable than previous decades — especially in the northern and eastern areas of the 
wheatbelt.  
Kingwell et al (2013)1 found that nearly two-thirds of a diverse set of 249 farm businesses across  
south-west WA (which includes the Wheatbelt) were classed as ‘growing’ or ‘strong’ despite a decade 
(2002–2011) of challenging weather years (see Figure 1). 
Farm profitability improved over the study period despite no underlying 
improvement in terms of trade. 
The authors point out that this profitability was achieved through better use of existing technologies, 
including those that offered economies of scale and a switch to more cropping (wheat) in favour of 
livestock (sheep).

7. Primary industries — building drought resilience 
188
“
“Farmers whose businesses grew strongly over the 
study period also displayed (on average) superior 
management capabilities and choices in many areas 
of farm management and were often more connected 
to their local community and achieved greater  
work-life balance.”
Kingwell et al (2013)2
In work commissioned by the Wheatbelt Development Commission for the 
Wheatbelt RDRP, farm consultancy group Planfarm discuss the period from 
2010 to 2014 (which delivered three seasons of drought or below-average 
rainfall across much of the Wheatbelt), and the 2018 and 2019 seasons for 
which frost was an issue in some areas and low rainfall in others. During this 
period, farms were also impacted by rising input costs and falling commodity 
prices. Despite the difficult seasons, the best performing Planfarm clients 
improved their productivity (water use efficiency) by about two kilograms of 
wheat per millimetre of rainfall (Figure 2).
In a broader benchmark survey of Wheatbelt farms between 2018–2024,  
75% of Planfarm clients were rated as having ‘high profit-high equity’ and only 
0.5% were rated ‘high profit-low equity’. About a fifth (21%) of those surveyed 
were rated as ‘low profit-high equity’ and 3.5% were in a vulnerable ‘low profit-
low equity’ position (Figure 3). Interestingly, most of the poorer performing 
farms were in medium to high rainfall zones, and not low rainfall areas.

The top 25% of Planfarm clients in the north-eastern wheatbelt

7. Primary industries — building drought resilience 
Image courtesy: Angie Roe, Austockphoto

192
7. Primary industries — building drought resilience 
The Planfarm authors determined that the best performing businesses 
had used their profits to improve soil condition and acquire machinery 
that enabled sowing time to be optimised for productivity “Their focus is 
on increasing profits to acquire additional land, which has stretched their 
advantage beyond the bulk of the group.” The report authors conclude that 
while farm business equity (brought about largely through significant 
increases in land values over the past decade) “has, to some degree, 
increased drought resilience, unprofitable businesses cannot rely on 
increasing asset values for resilience. It continues to be important for farm 
businesses to create and retain profits to manage the financial pressures  
of drought.” 
To date, the Wheatbelt has not experienced more than 
two consecutive years of drought across the entire 
region (Figure 4) but most climate models predict that 
southern Australia (and therefore the Wheatbelt) is at 
increased risk of drought in coming decades.3 
Farm equity of two-thirds of 120 studied farms in the northern and eastern 
grain growing areas of WA (which includes part of the Wheatbelt’s Central 
East sub-region) declined following the consecutive low rainfall years 
of 2006 and 2007 however, more than half of the farms had more than 
80% equity to start with so were able to absorb the poor seasons. Farms 
that remained resilient, despite the serious droughts were those “that 
cropped more than 50% of their farm area, were prudent in their expenditure, 
maintained some enterprise diversity and often generated wheat yields in 
each year that were near the yield potential for that year”.4
In another study investigating characteristics of farm business success 
during dry seasons, Kingwell et al (2013)5 state that ‘Growing and strong 
farms, when compared to less secure or secure farms have, on average, 
made greater use of leasing, contractors, superannuation funds, succession 
planning, Farm Management Deposits (FMDs), and off-farm assets. They also, 
on average, adopt and make greater use of farm business software, marketing 
strategies, decision support tools, precision technology, electronic paddock 
recording and GPS technology’.
3.	 Grose, M. R., Narsey, S., Delage, F. P., Dowdy, A. J., Bador, M., Boschat, G., et al. (2020). Insights from CMIP6 for Australia’s future climate. Earth’s Future, 8
4.	 Lawes, R., and Kingwell, R. (2012) A longitudinal examination of business performance indicators for drought-affected farms, Agricultural Systems, Volume 106, Issue 1
5.	 Kingwell, R, Anderton, L, Islam, N, Xayavong, V, Wardell-Johnson, A, Feldman, D & Speijers, J 2013, Broadacre farmers adapting to a changing climate, National Climate Change Adaptation Research 
Facility, Gold Coast, 171 pp

194
7. Primary industries — building drought resilience 
In the very low rainfall seasons of 2018 and 2019, many southern  
wheatbelt properties almost ran out of water, causing a record destocking  
of sheep from the region (Figure 5). Without the rain that fell in 2021  
(see Figure 4) many farmers would have been forced to completely  
destock their properties. 
Building drought resilience in the broadacre livestock 
sector will require more efficient dams and scenario 
planning to ensure farms are self-sufficient in water 
during dry seasons.
To date, broadacre farms across the Wheatbelt have adapted well to a 
drying climate — due to adoption of new genetics, improved crop and 
livestock management, amelioration of soil constraints, economies of scale 
and machinery and technology advances. 
However, the gap between realised and potential grain yields is beginning to 
close (Figure 6) and new farming technologies and systems will be required 
to lift yield potential and capture and store more water for livestock as the 
climate continues to dry and seasons become more variable.
Image courtesy: Megan Hele

How do the top 25% of Wheatbelt farmers in the low 
rainfall zone manage seasonal and market variability? 
Can their approach and experience be transferred 
to higher rainfall areas in the Wheatbelt facing less 
rainfall and more variable seasons as the climate 
continues to dry?
Farmanco farm management consultant Rob Sands characterises 
top-performing agricultural businesses as:
•	 Understanding the profit drivers of their farming system that 
increase water use efficiency.
•	 Being uncompromising in achieving the right timing, rate, and 
product for every situation.
•	 Achieving each farming operation more quickly, more efficiently, 
and at a lower cost.
•	 Keeping a tight control on all costs.
•	 Buying and leasing more land based on performance and not  
on rainfall alone.

and often includes 
unobservable 
attributes that

8. Community resilience
Wheatbelt  
socio-economic status
Socio-economic 
determinants of 
community resilience are 
varied and complex and 
it is beyond the scope of 
this report to explore in 
detail how they play out 
across the Wheatbelt.2 
Drought and the effects of climate change will (and do) exacerbate the vulnerabilities of Wheatbelt 
communities through their impacts on water supplies, maintenance of green spaces for sport and recreation, 
mental health, excessive heat and extreme weather events, and the economic viability of the region’s major 
industry — broadacre agriculture.3
Figure 1 outlines the 2021 Australian Bureau of Statistics socio-economic (SEIFA) scores of all 42 Wheatbelt 
LGAs (900-1050) and compares these with the Wheatbelt (980) and state (1020) averages. 
About 55% of the 42 Wheatbelt LGAs fall below the WA average for  
socio-economic score while about 20% sit at or above the state average.

206
8. Community resilience
Drivers of community 
drought resilience
Understanding and 
quantifying the factors 
that drive drought and 
climate change resilience 
across the Wheatbelt 
is critical to developing 
policies and procedures 
that safeguard the 
region’s capacity to 
adapt to a drier, more 
variable climate. 
At a farm-level, drought resilience and climate change adaptation has, and is, receiving considerable focus 
— through federal, state and local research, development and extension. However, at the local government 
level there has been significantly less work done to understand and quantify the factors driving drought and 
climate change vulnerability and resilience in local communities.
Consultation with local governments as part of the Wheatbelt RDRP process has revealed a significant and 
comprehensive issue at the LGA level in terms of water for green spaces during dry years and concern in 
some LGAs around the growing competition for water between towns and renewable energy and mining 
developments.  
Green spaces hold significant value in Wheatbelt communities — especially those associated with sport and 
recreation, where communities gather socially to connect and support one another. Community participation 
in organised sport and recreation contributes to what is known as ‘social capital’ — the social fabric, or glue 
that ties members together in a given locality4. Maintaining green ovals and sporting grounds is seen as a 
priority for many Wheatbelt communities — even during drought when the costs of using scheme water can 
add tens of thousands of dollars to local council expenditure.
4.	 Cox, E., & Australian Broadcasting Corporation. (1995). A truly civil society / Eva Cox. ABC Books.
“
“Last year we had to switch to scheme water (to water the town oval) in early 
April and it cost us $20,000. Other shires without recycled wastewater systems 
are paying $50,000 to $100,000 or more each year to irrigate their ovals.”
Peter Stubbs, CEO, Williams Shire

no sport, people have to travel. And when you have 
water, people stay. That’s why it’s important.”
Dave Williams, Desalination Project Manager, Dumbleyung Shire

Carrying out LGA-level audits of water supply and demand would provide 
the baseline data needed for local communities to plan for their water needs 
into the future. However, capacity building and funding at the LGA level will 
be needed to achieve this — as local shires have little to no hydrological, 
engineering and program management expertise to carry out such 
assessments.

it’s impacting agriculture and our towns — but we are

to take a step back and look at things across the board, 
at a macro strategic level, and try and come up with a 
plan to understand and respond to our water shortfall.”
Gavin Treasure, CEO, Dumbleyung Shire

8. Community resilience
Case study
Ageing Wheatbelt swimming pools face uncertain future
210
8. Community resilience
About half of the 39 pools across the Wheatbelt have now exceeded 
the average 50-year lifespan for aquatic facilities. Most Wheatbelt 
pools are owned by local governments, which are responsible for 
their maintenance and replacement.  
The cost of upgrading pools often exceeds the budgets of small regional 
councils, leading to delays or neglect of essential repairs and replacements. 
As a result, many Wheatbelt shires are facing significant financial strain to 
maintain or upgrade their pools — putting the future of these community 
assets at serious risk of closure. 
The State of Australian Aquatic Facilities 2025 report states that for each 
dollar spent on pool operations and maintenance $5.46 is returned in 
social value through the support of healthier, safer and more connected 
communities. The first of four recommendations arising from the report is to 
“establish a National Public Pool Investment Program and secure sustainable, 
dedicated funding to upgrade, expand, and replace aging aquatic facilities”5. 
At a ratio of one aquatic facility per 2,013 people, the Wheatbelt has a 
relatively high number of swimming pools compared to its population: 
reflecting the vast geographic spread of its 200+ communities and a strong 
historical investment in aquatic infrastructure across the region. Over the past 
“
“Community hubs, like sport and recreation centres, play 
an important role during trying times. Communities need 
safe places and spaces to gather during drought.”
Wheatbelt RDRP consultancy feedback
5.	 Houston, R., Koon, W., & Scarr, J. (2025). State of Australian Aquatic Facilities 2025: Benchmarking Social, 
Health & Economic Value, Access Equity & Sustainability. Royal Life Saving Society — Australia, Sydney.
Image courtesy: Shire of Dumbleyung

decade, the average water consumption per patron visit at Wheatbelt pools 
was 567 litres — the highest rate of any region in the state and significantly 
higher than the state average of 99 litres per patron visit. 
Adopting water and energy-smart technologies and addressing the issues 
associated with aging infrastructure will be critical to ensuring local 
governments can continue to operate and manage their aquatic centres 
across the Wheatbelt. The Water Corporation’s ‘Waterwise Aquatic Centre 
Program’, which started in 2012, enables aquatic centres across Western 
Australia to be recognised for their commitment to water efficiency. The 
program includes 58 participants, 27 of these being in the Perth Metropolitan 
area and 31 in regional Western Australia. To date, there are only eight water 
wise aquatic facilities in the Wheatbelt.
In recent years, nearly $3 million in funding for Wheatbelt pool refurbishment 
has been made available through the state government’s Community Sport 
8. Community resilience
211
and Recreation Facilities Fund, which remains a valuable opportunity for 
local governments to secure funding support for community pool upgrades. 
Wheatbelt local governments have also had recent success in securing 
funding for pools from the federal government through the Growing Regions 
Program — with the shires of Cunderdin and Dumbleyung receiving about  
$4.5 million between them to redevelop their aquatic facilities.
Developing an audit of Wheatbelt pools in terms of 
age, condition, water and power use across the region’s 
LGAs and linking local communities with funding 
opportunities for pool refurbishments and efficiency 
upgrades would help secure these important social 
and health hubs across the region — especially as the 
climate dries and becomes hotter. 
Image courtesy: Shire of Moora

212
8. Community resilience
What is community 
resilience?
Quantifying and 
understanding 
resilience are complex 
tasks — and it is difficult 
to generate a single 
measure of resilience to 
compare communities.6
Community resilience is best understood as a dynamic process of adaptation and recovery rather than a 
static outcome — it is built steadily, through coordinated action and policies and is underpinned by strong 
social cohesion and connectedness. 
A community is considered resilient when ‘members of its population are 
connected to one another and work together, so that they are able to function 
and sustain critical systems, even under stress; adapt to changes in the 
physical, social or economic environment; be self-reliant if external resources 
are limited or cut off; and learn from experience to improve itself over time’.7 
Many inspirational examples of the Arbon et al (2016)7 community resilience definition can be found across 
the Wheatbelt’s communities and local government authorities (LGAs) — from individuals banding together 
to buy local grocery shops and pubs on behalf of their towns through to local government alliances that 
build agency and capacity, innovative aged care and childcare systems developed using local expertise; 
locally-developed wireless telecommunication systems; primary industries based on First Nation’s cultural 
and environmental values; and the many thousands of committed, long-term volunteers underpinning the 
region’s sporting, fire-fighting, ambulance and social clubs. 
6.	 How. G, & Houghton, K. (2021). Reviewing Indices of Resilience and Adaptability. Canberra, The Regional Australia Institute.
7.	 Arbon, P., Steenkamp, M., Cornell, V., Cusack, L. & Gebbie, K. (2016). Measuring disaster resilience in communities and households: 
Pragmatic tools developed in Australia. International Journal of Disaster Resilience in the Built Environment. 7. 201-215.

8. Community resilience
213
The WA government has recently committed an additional $2.1 million 
(to 2028) towards the state’s Volunteering Development Services (VDS) 
program, established in 2001 to ‘develop the capability of individuals, 
community groups and organisations to respond to their communities’ needs 
through volunteer involvement.’ Part of the VDS funding will go towards 
developing the Town Team Movement in the Wheatbelt — a WA initiative that 
works collaboratively with local governments to improve a place or area. 
A Town Team is a group of locals — businesses, residents, landowners 
and community members — working together with their local government 
to make their town or main street more vibrant, inclusive and connected. 
There are currently 179 Town Teams across WA with a dozen or so across 
the Wheatbelt. The North East Wheatbelt Region of Council (NEWROC) 
has recently partnered with the Town Team Movement to fund a Wheatbelt 
Town Team Builder — a dedicated person working with local governments 
and communities across the region to “help activate town centres, build local 
capacity, and bring ideas to life.”

214
8. Community resilience
Case study
Wheatbelt volunteer workforce largest in WA
The Wheatbelt Region has the highest volunteering rate of all WA 
regions — with nearly 25% of residents involved in volunteer work of 
some kind compared to 16% for the state average (Figure 2).
Volunteers underpin the sporting, social and emergency services across the 
Wheatbelt but the region’s ageing and slow-growing population presents 
challenges for both volunteer supply and demand. Volunteer burnout is 
considered a major risk facing rural volunteer organisations.
Holmes et al (2019)8 conclude that there is a need to better understand the 
type, frequency and intensity of volunteer participation across regional WA 
and that volunteer organisations need support to develop strategic plans so 
they can better manage the threats and opportunities facing the changing 
environment of the volunteering sector. 
Drought years and ongoing climate change will continue to place pressure 
on volunteer services in the Wheatbelt — particularly fire and emergency 
services but also social and sporting services. Capacity building and strategic 
planning will be crucial to maintaining the viability of local volunteer 
organisations in the region.

8. Community resilience

BCEC Research Report No. 22/19

of rural communities in Western Australia. The past 
two decades have seen an increase in the community 
services delivered by volunteers and demand on the 
volunteer workforce has intensified. Yet, according to 
data collected by the Australian Bureau of Statistics over 
the same period there has been a decline in volunteer 
participation across Australia, resulting in a shortage  
of volunteer labour.”8

8. Community resilience
216
Characteristics of  
a resilient community
The resilience of many 
Wheatbelt towns is 
under pressure from 
social, economic and 
infrastructure challenges 
— and many of these 
will (and already have) 
become more acute in the 
face of climate change 
and drought years. 
Each Wheatbelt LGA faces its own particular set of drought- and climate-related challenges; with some 
lacking infrastructure and services and others lacking human capital or economic diversity.  
Building an effective drought resilience plan for the Wheatbelt requires an 
ongoing and iterative process that unpacks and addresses the vulnerabilities 
of each LGA, while also identifying ways to better link and enhance services, 
economies, infrastructure and environmental assets within and across  
sub-regions.
Image: Darkan Hotel Community Project

8. Community resilience
217
217
217
8. Community resilience
The International Red Cross has identified 11 dimensions  
of a resilient community:
1.	
Risk management: a resilient community knows and  
manages its risks.
2.	 Health: a resilient community is healthy — mentally and physically.
3.	 Water and sanitation: a resilient community can meet its basic 
water and sanitation needs.
4.	 Shelter: a resilient community can meet its basic shelter needs.
5.	 Food and nutrition security: a resilient community can meet its 
basic food needs.
6.	 Economic opportunities: a resilient community has diverse 
economic opportunities.
7.	 Infrastructure and services: a resilient community has  
well-maintained and accessible infrastructure and services. 
8.	 Natural resource management: a resilient community has access 
to, manages and uses its natural assets in a sustainable manner. 
9.	 Social cohesion: a resilient community draws on formal and 
informal community networks to act for the good of all in the 
community. 
10.	Inclusion: a resilient community fights exclusion on any basis, 
including gender, ability, ethnicity, religion, political view etc. 
11.	 Connectedness: a resilient community has strong and supportive 
relationships with local government authorities and other external 
organisations, as well as access to information. 
How does the Wheatbelt region 
as a whole and its 42 LGAs, 200+ 
communities and 77,000 people fare 
in terms of these characteristics? And 
how do these characteristics play out 
in terms of drought resilience at a 
community, LGA and regional level? 
•	 Do Wheatbelt communities know and 
understand their drought risks? 
•	 How can resilience thinking be 
embedded into regional policy and 
planning?
•	 How can Wheatbelt schools, houses, 
green spaces and pools be optimised 
for liveability and sustainability as the 
climate dries and becomes hotter?
•	 Where is drought and community 
resilience being done well? And how 
can these success stories best be 
shared to foster pride and stronger 
resilience at a sub-regional and 
regional level?

218
8. Community resilience
Case study
Local leadership and commitment saves Wheatbelt social hub
218
8. Community resilience
“
“We need to empower Wheatbelt people to design  
and change their future — with a focus on collaboration, 
collective knowledge and working together.”
Wheatbelt RDRP consultancy feedback
In 2022, the small Wheatbelt community of Darkan, in the Shire 
of West Arthur, experienced a significant setback when the town’s 
only pub closed its doors. Built in 1906, the Darkan Hotel is a 
defining feature of the town’s character and visual identity and has 
long been regarded as the social hub of the community. The hotel is 
also a key economic contributor, serving as the cornerstone of the 
town’s tourism sector and providing valuable local employment. 
When the hotel’s lessee decided not to renew the lease, the business was 
listed for sale by the owners but the poor condition of the property — which 
had lacked repairs and maintenance over many years — did not attract any 
serious buyers.  Recognising the pivotal role of the hotel to the social and 
economic fabric of the town, and aware of the limited buyer interest, a small 
group of motivated residents formed a committee to oversee the project to 
purchase, restore and re-open the hotel. 
Advice was sought from a local lawyer and accountant on possible investment 
structures to fund the project. Following community consultation, a board 
for the project was formally elected and a consultant engaged to develop a 
clear vision and purpose and establish operational guidelines. A company was 
established and shares in a unit trust were sold to 42 community investors, 
raising over $760,000. Most investors were local farmers and residents, 
many with no previous experience in the hospitality industry. The project also 
received a $182,350 grant from the Regional Economic Development (RED) 
program to construct adjacent accommodation, addressing the shortage of 
short-stay options in the area. 
Beyond the initial financial investment and professional expertise, the 
project’s operation was driven by dedicated volunteers — from project 
planning and management to the hands-on labour involved in restoring 
the property. The elected board met frequently, overseeing all aspects of 
planning, finance, and communications. Regular updates were provided to 
keep both investors and the wider community informed. The refurbished 
Darkan Hotel reopened in September 2024, offering meals, drinks, and 
accommodation. The revival of the pub not only restored a cherished social 
venue but also stimulated local economic activity.

8. Community resilience
Image courtesy: Darkan Hotel

220
8. Community resilience
Case study
Regional resilience compromised by vulnerable infrastructure
220
8. Community resilience
“
“The telecommunications network is already  
sub-standard and heavily impacted by seasonal 
events such as summer storms. Online connectivity 
is essential for individuals in rural communities for 
health, work, education and social connectedness. 
We need an infrastructure plan for drought resilience 
across the region.”
Wheatbelt RDRP consultancy feedback
The North Eastern Wheatbelt Region of Councils (NEWROC) is a 
voluntary alliance of seven local governments in the north-eastern 
Wheatbelt formed to strengthen regional cooperation. NEWROC 
brings together the shires of Dowerin, Koorda, Mount Marshall, 
Mukinbudin, Nungarin, Trayning and Wyalkatchem (Figure 3). 
A key focus of the NEWROC alliance is securing essential infrastructure 
capacity for the region.
In January 2024, a severe storm struck WA’s Wheatbelt and Goldfields regions, 
causing a widespread and extended power outage that impacted more than 
34,000 households and businesses9. The storm caused major damage to 
critical infrastructure, including the collapse of transmission towers and 
disruption to essential services such as water supply, healthcare, fuel access, 
and telecommunications. 
Power was not fully restored for nine days.
The outage raised serious concerns about the long-term 
resilience of infrastructure in regional and remote areas 
in the face of increasing severe storms linked to climate 
change.
NEWROC engaged economics consultants Econosis to undertake an impact 
assessment of the storm-induced power outage. The analysis revealed a loss 
of $11.1 million in economic output over just six days. This consisted of $4.6m 
in lost local economic outputs, followed by $1.1m in first round and supply 
chain impacts and a further $5.4m in production and consumption impacts.
Wheatbelt doctor Michael Livingston said when the power first went off at his 
Narembeen practice he started seeing patients in the dark but after losing 
phone and internet access was unable to proceed. The blackout is estimated 
to have cost the practice more than $20,000, partly because vaccines and 
medicines were spoiled.9  
9.	 https://www.abc.net.au/news/2024-01-19/kalgoorlie-blackout-explainer/103365870

8. Community resilience
221
DOWERIN
WYALKATCHEM
KOORDA
MOUNT MARSHALL
TRAYNING
NUNGARIN
MUKINBUDIN
The scale of loss from just a single outage — exacerbated by 
climate volatility — underpins the need for urgent investment 
in resilient energy and communication systems across the 
Wheatbelt.
The Australian Energy Market Operator has warned of increased 
risks to national power supply due to aging infrastructure, 
extreme weather events, and the complexities of integrating 
renewable energy.10 If outages continue annually at current rates, 
the projected economic loss for the NEWROC region could reach 
$125.9 million in present value terms over 20 years (based on a 
six-day annual outage).
NEWROC has taken proactive steps to mitigate outage impacts 
and build local resilience.
In collaboration with local Wheatbelt company Crisp Wireless, 
NEWROC has expanded a telecommunications network capable 
of operating independently of the mains power grid. During the 
January outage, this system enabled continued internet access 
and phone connectivity for those connected — demonstrating the 
value of locally led solutions.
NEWROC has also submitted two market-led proposals, applying 
for federal funding, and engaging with Western Power and state 
energy ministers, however their calls for broader infrastructure 
investment in the north-eastern Wheatbelt have yet to be 
matched by state or national commitments.

8. Community resilience
222
8. Community resilience
• 	 Complete a region-wide ‘resilient green spaces’ audit
• 	 Deliver a ‘Resilient Wheatbelt’ small grants scheme to fund locally-driven drought resilience initiatives
• 	 Develop and deliver a small business climate resilience program
• 	 In conjunction with the Wheatbelt District Leadership Group, implement climate resilience actions from the 
‘Living Well in the Wheatbelt’ (2025–2035) strategy
• 	 Develop and communicate Wheatbelt resilience stories  to enhance awareness of local leaders taking proactive 
steps to build resilience in their communities, industry and region
• 	 Leverage existing leadership networks and programs to build capacity of Wheatbelt leaders and embed 
resilience thinking and climate scenarios into strategic planning and policy development

9. Regional resilience
Introduction
The Wheatbelt’s 
ongoing resilience 
will be underpinned 
by local leaders 
working collectively 
to future-proof the 
region’s economic, 
environmental and 
social success in the 
face of a drying climate.
This section places a drought lens over each of the five resilience measures listed above and highlights what 
is working well in the Wheatbelt and what is needed to strengthen drought resilience across the region.
Regional resilience (which includes drought resilience) is underpinned by:
•	 Good governance
•	 Economic diversity 
•	 Quality infrastructure — housing, communications, energy, transport and water
•	 Availability and interconnectedness of support services and social networks 
•	 Valued and healthy natural environment
“
“A resilient Wheatbelt embraces innovation, diversity and collaboration 
— enabling communities and industries to withstand external pressures 
while continuing to grow.”
Wheatbelt RDRP consultancy feedback

9. Regional resilience

9. Regional resilience

228
9. Regional resilience
Wheatbelt 
governance
The Wheatbelt region 
is classified into five 
sub-regions: Avon, 
Central Coast, Wheatbelt 
South, Central East and 
Central Midlands and has 
42 local governments 
overseeing communities 
varying from fewer than 
200 to 11,000 people. 
Within the Wheatbelt, the Wheatbelt Development Commission (WDC) represents the state government and 
Regional Development Australia (RDA) represents the federal government. The WDC and RDA play key roles in 
economic and community development across the Wheatbelt and both organisations engage closely with the 
region’s LGAs. 
Wheatbelt Natural Resource Management (Wheatbelt NRM) is a not-for-profit group established to address 
environmental challenges across the Wheatbelt. 
The Ballardong, Yued and Gnala Karla Boodja Aboriginal corporations manage and maximise opportunities for 
aboriginal people across the Noongar estate in the region. 
A range of sub-regional forums exist that bring Wheatbelt LGAs and regional organisations together to discuss 
common issues, support advocacy and problem solving:
WA Local Government Authority (WALGA) Zones —  Central Country, Great Eastern and Avon-Midland 
Zones are sub-regional bodies that provide a framework to facilitate input on key matters impacting their local 
and regional areas into WALGA policy formulation. WALGA representatives are often called upon to represent 
their regions on regional level committees or groups, including the Wheatbelt District Leadership Group.
228
Image courtesy: Shire of Merredin

229
9. Regional resilience
229
Regional Organisation of Councils (ROCs) and Regional Alliances — 
not-for-profit collaborations of local governments focussed on economic 
growth and community development in specific geographic areas of the 
Wheatbelt. These groups undertake federal and state government advocacy 
and strategic planning work in service delivery (e.g. aged care, housing, 
communications), tourism development, natural resource management and 
land use planning:
•	
Avon ROC — Toodyay, Goomalling, Northam, York and Victoria Plains
•	
Wheatbelt East ROC — Bruce Rock, Cunderdin, Kellerberrin, Merredin, 
Tammin, Westonia and Yilgarn
•	
North Eastern Wheatbelt ROC — Dowerin, Koorda, Mount Marshall, 
Mukinbudin, Nungarin, Trayning and Wyalkatchem.
•	
4WDL ROC — Wagin, West Arthur, Williams, Woodanilling, Dumbleyung  
and Lake Grace
•	
South East Avon ROC — Beverley, Cunderdin, Quairading, York 
•	
Hotham-Williams Economic Development Alliance — Boddington, 
Wandering and Williams 
•	
Northern Growth Alliance — Chittering, Dandaragan and  Gingin
Topic specific groups exist to progress key opportunities such as tourism 
(e.g. NEWTRAVEL and ROE Tourism), health (Rural Local Government Health 
Alliance), housing (Central East Accommodation and Care Alliance), transport 
(Wheatbelt Secondary Freight Network) and water (Rural Water Council).
These forums are essential in building collective advocacy and critical mass to 
attract attention and investment into place-based solutions.
While the region’s well connected governance system has fostered innovative responses to complex 
challenges, maintaining this capacity in the long term will be critical in the face of population pressures 
on services and resources, climate change, and a gradual consolidation of the agricultural industry, all 
of which have impacted the region’s ability to attract and retain skilled professionals and services. 
Examples of successful collaborative projects undertaken  
across the Wheatbelt include:
•	 The Wheatbelt Secondary Freight Network (WSFN) involving 
all 42 Wheatbelt LGAs. The WSFN attracted funding to improve 
road safety and efficiency across 4400 kilometres of high-
priority, locally managed routes in the region. 
•	 The Central East Accommodation and Care Alliance (11 LGAs) 
successfully built a case and attracted funding to build 71 
independent living units across their communities and will be 
building on this success by adding another 54 units in coming 
years. 
•	 The Wheatbelt Regional Housing initiative (10 LGAs)  
recently attracted funding to develop 200 lots in preparation  
for 400 new houses.

230
9. Regional resilience
Economic 
diversity
The Wheatbelt’s 
diversification index 
currently reflects 
a moderate level of 
economic diversity — with 
significant strengths in 
agriculture and mineral 
sands mining — and 
emerging opportunities in 
downstream processing 
of agricultural and mining 
commodities, renewable 
energy, biofuels, and 
manufacturing hubs.1
Dryland agriculture and horticulture underpin the Wheatbelt’s economy and employment — generating nearly 
a third of the region’s annual economic output, employing nearly a quarter of all employed and responsible 
for 45% of all businesses (Figure 1). Wheatbelt mining operations generate about the same annual economic 
output as agriculture and create economic diversity for the region — but mining employs 75% fewer people 
and accounts for just 1% of all Wheatbelt businesses (Figure 1). 
Lifting drought resilience across the Wheatbelt’s agricultural and  
horticultural industries will be critical to the ongoing economic success  
of the region. 
Drought compromises the viability of dryland agricultural enterprises through its impacts on rainfall for 
annual crops and water supply for broadacre livestock production. Lawes and Kingwell (2012)2 found that 
consecutive drought years in 2006 and 2007 greatly challenged the profitability of farm businesses in 
the northeast (low rainfall) part of WA’s grainbelt (which includes parts of the eastern Wheatbelt) — with 
almost two-thirds of farms in this area experiencing a decline in business equity between 2004 and 2009. 
Such declines in equity have obvious flow on effects to local communities in terms of money spent on local 
businesses and population dynamics. 
1.	 Economic Diversification in the Wheatbelt (2025) Bankwest Curtin Economic Centre
2.	 Lawes, R. & Kingwell, R. (2012) A longitudinal examination of business performance indicators for drought-affected farms, 
Agricultural Systems, Volume 106, Issue 1.

9. Regional resilience
231
3.	 Kingwell, R, Anderton, L, Islam, N, Xayavong, V, Wardell-Johnson, A, Feldman, D & Speijers, J 2013, Broadacre farmers adapting to a changing climate, National Climate Change 
Adaptation Research Facility, Gold Coast, 171 pp.
4.	 Grose, M. R., Narsey, S., Delage, F. P., Dowdy, A. J., Bador, M., Boschat, G., et al. (2020). Insights from CMIP6 for Australia’s future climate. Earth’s Future, 8.
However, in other research across the entire southwest agricultural area 
of WA (which includes the whole of the Wheatbelt), Kingwell et al (2013)3 
found that nearly two-thirds of a diverse set of 249 farm businesses were 
classed as growing or strong despite a decade (2002–2011) of challenging 
weather years underpinned by a warming and drying trend. Farm profitability 
improved over the study period despite no underlying improvement in terms 
of trade. This profitability was achieved through better use of existing 
technologies, including those that offered economies of scale and a switch 
to more cropping (wheat) in favour of livestock (sheep). Farmers whose 
businesses grew strongly over the study period also displayed (on average) 
superior management capabilities and choices in many areas of farm 
management and were often more connected to their local community and 
achieved greater work-life balance. 
To date, the Wheatbelt has not experienced more than two consecutive 
years of drought across the entire region but most climate models indicate 
an increased prevalence of drought and southern Australia, in particular, is 
predicted to be at increased risk of drought in coming decades.4  
Encouraging greater diversity in the Wheatbelt economy 
while continuing to develop and implement drought 
resilience measures in agricultural industries will be 
critical to bolstering the region’s drought resilience.

9. Regional resilience
Infrastructure
Harnessing the 
Wheatbelt’s full 
economic potential will 
require intervention 
and effort to overcome 
key barriers including 
housing availability, 
and transport and other 
enabling infrastructure 
networks including digital 
connectivity, power, and 
water across the region.
Housing
Lack of housing for workers is currently seen as one the most pressing barriers to business success in 
the Wheatbelt — with about 50% of respondents to the Chamber of Commerce and Industry WA (CCIWA) 
business confidence survey indicating housing shortages were thwarting their ability to attract and retain 
staff compared to 20% of all WA respondents (Figure 2)5. The Wheatbelt Development Commission is working 
with 32 of the Wheatbelt’s 42 local governments to analyse housing needs across the region.
About 60% of businesses surveyed (representing 16% of all Wheatbelt 
businesses) indicated that they require, on average, 1.4 additional workers to 
meet current needs. This amounts to a demand for more than  
1,300 houses to 2031. 
A lack of serviced land, high costs and availability of builders and construction trades are major barriers to 
the development of new housing stock in the Wheatbelt.6 
Work completed by the WDC with the 4WDL Regional Organisation of Councils in 2024 identified a 
shortfall of $50,000 to $90,000 between the costs of lot development and their developed market value, 
representing significant market failure. The opportunity cost of not having housing for critical workers was 
estimated at $265,000 per worker.7

6.	 JE Planning Services WEROC housing analysis, 2025.
7.	 JE Planning Service and Chadwick Consulting (2024) 4WDL Local housing plans and key worker housing investment concepts 
Technical Appendix to 4WDL Preliminary Business Case — Key Worker Housing 2023–2024.

Wheatbelt LGAs are working collaboratively to address housing  
challenges. For example, in 2025, led by the Shire of Victoria Plains, a 
group of 10 Wheatbelt LGAs attracted funding from the Federal Government 
Housing Support Program for up to 200 residential lots with the capacity 
to support 400 new houses. In the Central East Wheatbelt sub-region, the 
Central East Aged Care Alliance (CEACA) is adding 54 independent living 
units to its 71-unit development across 11 LGAs in the eastern Wheatbelt. 
A combination of low rates of land development 
and housing construction, together with a growing 
demand for housing, has caused a severe shortage 
of housing in regional WA.

9. Regional resilience
Digital connectivity
Mobile phone coverage and internet connectivity remain overriding issues for 
businesses and households in the Wheatbelt — with each of the region’s LGAs 
falling below the average ‘digital inclusion index’ for Australia (Figure 3).
Internet connectivity in the Wheatbelt currently occurs via Sky Muster, ADSL 
or fixed wireless services and mobile Wi-Fi. However, the growing number 
of Sky Muster users has resulted in reduced data speeds and limited data 
allowances. To address poor data speeds and allowances, local solutions 
using privately owned base stations or fibre optic connections have emerged 
(e.g., Crisp Wireless). Introduction of satellite technology through Starlink is 
also showing potential to help meet the demand for better connectivity.
Telstra has predicted there will be a 20–30% year-on-year growth in demand 
for data in Australia until 2031.8 
Telecommunications infrastructure now drives 
economic development and underpins social connection 
and community safety. With data storage for most 
businesses having moved to the cloud, access to higher 
data speeds and reliable internet connections has 
become essential. 
Access to connectivity is also critical to ongoing productivity of the 
Wheatbelt’s key industry — agriculture. The uptake of digital technologies by 
the agricultural sector is estimated to increase gross value of production by 
more than $20 billion annually across Australia. Farm machinery automation, 
edge of field and remote monitoring, traceability technology and drone usage 
all require access to quality reliable internet.9  
WA’s Digital Industries Acceleration Strategy10 aligns with and supports the 
state’s economic framework Diversify WA and identifies a raft of projects 
aimed at accelerating digital transformation and uplifting WA businesses and 
industries. Efforts are also underway to improve regional connectivity through 
national and state programs such as the NBN upgrades, the Mobile Black Spot 
Program, the Regional Connectivity Program, and the Mobile Network Hardening 
Program. In addition, digital advancements, such as the Telstra intercity 
dark fibre network offering ultra-fast speeds of up to 83.6 terra bytes per 
second will position towns like Northam and Merredin as hubs for data-driven 
industries.
However, while these initiatives are making a difference — particularly in areas 
close to Perth and major regional centres — their impact can be uneven, with 
many Wheatbelt businesses still facing challenges like poor coverage, satellite 
congestion, and long wait times for new services.11

Telecommunications investment is influenced by population density 
and delivery of new and upgraded mobile coverage is prioritised 
where population densities are highest. But many Wheatbelt LGAs 
have small and even declining populations — making access to 
services and infrastructure increasingly challenging.
“In rural and remote settings, the cost of providing telecommunications 
infrastructure increases, and the returns reduce, as population densities 
decline. In some cases, this limits the scope for universal coverage by 
commercially-focused private sector operators, without government 
intervention” — Australian Infrastructure Audit (2019).12

9. Regional resilience
Case study
Wireless telecommunications 
maintain connections
In 2017, NEWROC successfully applied for Building Better Regions 
Funding from the federal government ($701,875) to partner with a 
WA business (Crisp Wireless) to install a data centre and 11 repeater 
stations, operated by solar power, across NEWROC’s seven  
local governments. 
The Crisp Wireless network provides a reliable internet service and is also 
designed to be used for wireless phone calls over internet. Crisp Wireless was 
conceived by a group of local individuals who viewed the need for greater 
connectivity in the regions, with a local workforce capable of addressing 
issues in a timely manner through their close geographical network. 
NEWROC contributed $256,625 of their own funds to activate the project 
enabling the wireless communications network to be expanded to 15 repeater 
stations across the seven shires. In addition, neighbouring Wheatbelt 
shires (Merredin, Westonia, Yilgarn, Kellerberrin, Narembeen, Bruce Rock, 
Quairading, Dalwallinu) have also connected to the NEWROC wireless 
network by attracting state funds and matching these with their own local 
government funds. 
The new infrastructure has connected businesses and the wider community 
to enhanced opportunities for business diversification, tourism growth, online 
education and professional development, while reducing social isolation 
and ultimately making the area a better place to live, work and invest. Most 
importantly, the wireless network withstood the January 2024 storms that 
240
9. Regional resilience
caused six-days of telecommunication outages in the north-eastern Wheatbelt 
and was the only internet (and WiFi calling) service that did not experience 
downtime, because it was supplied by solar power and battery storage.

Local governments are generally not providers of 
utility services such as telecommunications but 
telecommunications services underpin the capacity 
of local governments to deliver their emergency

The supercell thunderstorm that occurred across the eastern Wheatbelt 
region in January 2024 highlighted the need for robust back-up systems 
to enhance regional telecommunications infrastructure at the shire level, 
ensuring that essential services and communication remain operational 
during future emergencies. In response to the storm event, and with strong 
advocacy from NEWROC, the WA Government and Telstra invested $2.9M 
in the Automated Transfer Unit Pilot Program, that resulted in 80 ATUs being 
installed across strategic Telstra mobile towers and exchanges across the 
Wheatbelt and western Goldfields regions. 13

9. Regional resilience
242
Energy infrastructure
Access to reliable and affordable power is critical to the success of the 
Wheatbelt’s major industries — agriculture, mining and manufacturing — while 
also ensuring delivery of vital services such as telecommunications and water 
and ensuring the region’s liveability, especially during hot summer months. 
Western Australia’s energy system is becoming 
decentralised and more diverse with energy generation 
shifting from centrally derived thermal sources (coal and 
gas), to dispersed roof top solar, large-scale wind and 
solar and battery storage. 
Renewable energy now accounts for about 38% of the electricity entering the 
South West Interconnected System (SWIS) network compared with just 10% a 
decade ago and will play an increasing role as the state transitions to net zero 
emissions by 2050 (Figure 4). 
The SWIS is WA’s main electricity network, serving 1.2 million customers 
across the South West Land Division, including the entire Wheatbelt, and 
comprises more than 7,750 kilometres of transmission lines (Figure 5) and a 
further 90,000+ kilometres of distribution lines. The SWIS is a self-contained 
system, which means it is not interconnected with other electricity networks 
and therefore has no outside support or backup. 
Image courtesy: BIA Energy Solutions

244
9. Regional resilience
The vast geographic reach of the SWIS means supply 
can be variable in the extremes of the network and the 
almost 800,000 wooden power poles create ongoing 
maintenance and operational issues. 
The SWIS was originally developed around centralised, large-scale electricity 
generation from coal-fired stations but with the move to decentralised 
renewable wind and solar energy production, and utility scale battery storage, 
major technical and infrastructure upgrades are required to integrate the new 
electricity sources into the network.
The Australian Energy Market Operator (AEMO) has identified that ongoing 
investment in electricity generation, battery storage and transmission 
infrastructure will be critical to managing WA’s growing electricity demand 
and offsetting the electricity losses incurred from retiring coal power stations. 
More than 4,000 kilometres of new high-capacity transmission lines and  
50 GW of new electricity generation (wind, solar, firming gas) and battery 
storage will be needed in the SWIS network over the next 20 years to 
accommodate the expected five-fold annual demand for electricity by 2042.14  
The scale of work required is unprecedented in the history of the SWIS.
To shore up power reliability across south-west WA (including the Wheatbelt), 
Western Power is overseeing the development of a ‘modular grid’ system to 
complement and in some cases replace the ‘poles and wires’ of the traditional 
SWIS grid. The modular grid will comprise a mix of microgrids, stand-alone 
power systems, battery storage systems and the existing transmission network. 
Much of the Wheatbelt sits within the so-called proposed ‘autonomous network’, 
which will be a mix of stand-alone power systems independent of the grid and 
microgrids integrated with the transmission network.
