246
9. Regional resilience
Case study
Wheatbelt power outages 
Reliability and resilience of power supplies in rural, regional and 
remote Western Australia is significantly worse than in urban and 
CBD areas. Data published by the WA state-owned Western Power 
corporation show the duration and frequency of power outages in 
rural areas were four to five times higher than in urban areas during 
2022–2023. 
Of the 10 poorest performing LGAs on the SWIS in 2022–2023, four were in 
the Wheatbelt where customers experienced 9 to 16 outages over the year 
with each outage lasting an average 18 to 69 hours in duration (Table 1). 
246
9. Regional resilience
Of the 10 worst performing shires, Quairading experienced 40% more outages 
than the average (16 days vs 11.5 days) with each outage more than double 
the average for duration (70 hours vs 30 hours). A five-day power outage in 
2022, which also affected fuel access and mobile phone reception, prompted 
the shire of Quairading to write to the State Energy Minister to seek a move 
to a microgrid for the town which would provide power sourced from a solar 
generator attached to a battery and enable the town to transition away from 
poles and wires. There are currently four microgrids in regional WA — Kalbarri, 
Perenjori, Bremer Bay and Ravensthorpe but none in the Wheatbelt. A WA 
parliamentary committee in 2020 found microgrids could provide significant 
benefits to regional communities including more reliable power and reduced 
bushfire risks.15  
Microgrids use local renewable generation and storage and remain connected 
to the SWIS network but have the ability to be ‘islanded’ as an autonomous 
system if the main grid goes down. Microgrids can also reduce maintenance 
costs for the SWIS by deferring — or entirely removing — the need to invest in 
costly pole and wire replacement programs. 
Western Power’s 2023–2024 reliability and power quality report outlines 
multiple severe weather events that impacted the SWIS network resulting 
in more than double the outages (>12 hours) of the previous year. About 
7000 customers, predominantly from the Wheatbelt, experienced more 
than 16 outages during the year, almost four times the outage frequency

9. Regional resilience
Renewable energy 
With conditions conducive to wind, solar and biomass generation, and high 
voltage transmission infrastructure traversing the region the Wheatbelt has 
significant prospects in renewable energy production, already contributing 
to over 50% of the state’s renewable energy from wind and solar farms. The 
region hosts the state’s largest wind farm with a strong pipeline of projects 
to expand capacity. Renewable fuels is also an emerging sub-sector, with 
Wheatbelt considered ideal for large scale production.16
The Wheatbelt region is critical to the transition of the SWIS to renewables. 
Wind and solar generation in the region is expected to grow to just under 
9000 MW over the next 15 years (95% wind and 5% solar). As of June 2024, 
the Wheatbelt region was generating about 1000 MW of renewable energy 
over eight operating projects with 22 additional projects in varying phases of 
feasibility, construction and development. Projects are clustered in zones in 
the Central Coast and Central Midlands sub-regions near the Clean Energy 
Link — North transmission line and in the Wheatbelt South near the Clean 
Energy Link — East transmission line. 
Operational projects include the Collgar Wind Farm (222 MW), Emu Downs 
Wind Farm (80 MW), Yandin Wind Farm (212 MW), Merredin Solar Farm  
(100 MW) and Cunderdin Hybrid Solar Battery Facility (128 MW). Projects 
in pre-construction and construction phases as of March 2026 include the 
King Rocks Wind Farm at Hyden (150 MW), Merredin Big Battery (100 MW) 
and Narrogin Solar Farm and BESS (200 MW). There are numerous projects 
in feasibility, planning and approval stages. Connection to the SWIS is 
dependent on upgrades to the transmission infrastructure.
WA’s transition to renewable energy offers significant economic and 
community benefits to Wheatbelt communities, including diversification, 
new industries, jobs, and localised growth. However, LGAs and private 
landholders are seeking guidance on how best to interact with developers — 
to capture opportunities and minimise negative imapcts. Cumulative impact 
of multiple projects on local services and infrastructure, community benefits 
and transparent planning are key concerns of those Wheatbelt LGAs in areas 
being targeted by renewable energy companies and Western Power.  
A review commissioned by federal energy minister Chris Bowen in 2023 
found that repeated instances of poor community engagement had led to a 
‘material distrust’ of renewable energy project developers and, in particular, 
developers of major new grid transmission projects. The review was based on 
more than 75 meetings with 700-plus participants and received more than 
500 submissions.17
16.  Infrastructure Australia (2022) Regional Strengths and Infrastructure Gaps report. Accessed 
10 November 2025: https://www.infrastructureaustralia.gov.au/2022-regional-strengths-and-
infrastructure-gaps-WA
17.  Andrew Dyer, Australian Energy Infrastructure Commissioner, 2023, Community Engagement

9. Regional resilience
Case study
Harnessing community benefits from Wheatbelt windfarms  
The Shire of West Arthur (Wheatbelt South sub-region) has been a 
focal point for renewable energy developers since April 2023. West 
Arthur is considered an ideal location for wind farm developments 
due to the ability of renewable energy generators to connect to the 
South West Interconnected System (SWIS) and its consistently 
positive wind test readings. Four large-scale windfarm renewable 
energy projects are currently in development within the shire  
(Table 2). 
Shire of West Arthur farmer and deputy Shire president Karen Harrington 
attended a national Regional Leaders Summit in August 2025 organised by the 
Renewable Energy Alliance (RE-Alliance) — an independent not-for-profit group 
helping regional communities and energy companies work together to ensure 
the shift to renewable energy delivers a lasting legacy to rural  
and regional Australia.
252
9. Regional resilience
Held in Newcastle, NSW, the summit was attended by 90 regional leaders 
from across Australia working both inside and outside local government — 
Shire staff and councillors and also not-for-profit and regional development 
organisations. A major focus of the two-day summit was determining how 
local governments and community groups could work to ensure community 
needs and aspirations are realised in the shift to renewable energy. 
“
“Wheatbelt communities have a central role to play in 
the renewable energy transition, but the resources and 
capacity of most local government authorities are not 
commensurate with the task at hand.”
Lu Allan, Regional Energy Alliance

253
“West Arthur Shire is a small local government with limited capacity. We need 
support from both state and federal government to resource our Shire to build 
capacity within our community before the construction phase. The potential 
benefits from the energy transition is on a scale that could set our shire up for 
the next generation.”
Karen wanted to attend the summit as she was aware of the amount of 
regional experience that would be in the room — and the lessons that could be 
shared to help West Arthur navigate through its own energy transition. 
“Learning from other communities at the summit really gave us some ideas — 
and the network we have now formed within WA will be really beneficial going 
forward as we transition to renewable energy.”
Karen came away from the summit with a goal of developing an energy plan 
and strategy for the West Arthur Shire and to establish ways to ensure that 
West Arthur benefits not only from hosting the projects but also from the 
manufacturing side of the renewable energy transition. 
“Our Shire CEO has reached out to the manufacturing people involved with 
the wind farms to see how we can build manufacturing capacity in our local 
government area and enable the community to benefit from the construction 
phase of the wind farms.”

Case study
Windfarm could deliver drought and community resilience  
254
9. Regional resilience
Wheatbelt windfarm developer and farmer Tim Stevenson’s initial 
motivation to enter the renewable energy industry was to drought-
proof his family’s Dandaragan farming operation in the Central 
Coast sub-region. 
But realising the capacity for the windfarm to have broader social impacts, his 
company Parron Developments invested early in a community framework that 
will ensure the proposed windfarm generates a financial legacy for his local 
shire. If approved, the Parron Windfarm has committed to the establishment 
of a community benefit fund that would see funding of $1,000 per MW  
per year of energy produced delivered to the Shire of Dandaragan over 
the 35-year life of the project — about $500,000 each year. The fund will 
establish appropriate governance including representatives from local 
government, traditional owners, community, and developers. Other community 
benefits will include the jobs created as part of the project, with a focus on 
local procurement, and funds to support local housing and partnerships. 
“
“The renewable energy transition will play out in  
20–30 LGAs across the Wheatbelt — how can we harness 
some of the economic value that will come from the 
transition to enhance our water, telecommunications, 
power, housing infrastructure and provide opportunities 
for local suppliers to build their businesses? The 
renewable energy transition offers huge opportunities for 
the Wheatbelt — but we need to plan and collaborate to 
generate the benefits for our local communities  
and natural ecosystems.”
Renee Manning Director, Wheatbelt Development Commission

regional benefits as well as clean energy for WA.

focus for upgraded infrastructure, providing more certainty for communities 
and developers. The state government has developed community benefit

9. Regional resilience
Transport infrastructure 
With more than 80% of Wheatbelt agricultural produce and a growing volume 
of mining commodities exported each year, road and rail infrastructure lie at 
the heart of the Wheatbelt’s economy. 
Broadacre grains (wheat, barley, canola, oats and pulses) generate nearly 
70% of the Wheatbelt’s annual agricultural value ($4.4 billion) — and account 
for most of the region’s agricultural freight and rail freight costs each year. 
Mining operations associated with the green energy sector are also expanding 
in the Wheatbelt with Chalice mining (Moora) alone expected to be producing 
and transporting 5–10 mega-tonnes of green metals per year along 
 Wheatbelt transport routes. 
256
Image courtesy: Ben White
Improving the efficiency with which agricultural and 
mining commodities and inputs are transported into and 
out of the Wheatbelt is critical to the ongoing resilience 
of the region’s economy.

9. Regional resilience
257
9. Regional resilience
257
The Wheatbelt’s road and rail freight network faces 
several challenges:
•	 ageing and under-performing road, rail and bridge 
infrastructure
•	 consolidation of grain receival sites (down from  
200 to 100 in the past 30 years) resulting in grain 
trucks having to travel further to deliver grain 
•	 price disparity between rail and road making it 
cheaper to transport grain by road 
•	 larger farm operations needing to move greater 
volumes of grain and machinery over larger distances
•	 closure of Tier-3 railway lines (in 2014) forcing grain 
to travel further by road to receival sites and port
•	 increasing number and use of larger vehicles on 
roads originally designed for much smaller load limits
•	 small rate-payer base of many Wheatbelt local 
government authorities charged with maintaining and 
upgrading road and bridge infrastructure

258
9. Regional resilience
The WA Government’s Revitalising Agricultural Region Freight Strategy 
(2020)20 emphasises the economic imperative of improving the efficiency 
and effectiveness of WA agricultural supply chains: 
Wheatbelt road network
The Wheatbelt contains about 30% (41,000 km) of WA’s total road network 
with the region’s 42 local government authorities responsible for about 
90% of the network and the state government responsible for funding and 
managing roads classed as ‘highways’ and ‘main roads’ (about 3,500 km in 
total) (Figure 6). 
Wheatbelt LGAs are charged with the responsibility for funding, planning, 
designing and operating the road networks and nearly all bridge structures in 
their local areas.
About 25% of local government Wheatbelt roads are sealed while 75% are 
unsealed (gravelled, formed or unformed). 
Just over 70% of the local Wheatbelt road network functions as ‘access roads’ 
— residential roads providing access to properties. A further 11% of the local 
network (about 4,700 km) functions as ‘regional distributor’ roads that link 
significant destinations within and outside the Wheatbelt. Regional distributor 
roads are critical for moving agricultural and mining freight. The remaining 
20% of the road network is known as ‘local distributers’ — roads that act in a 
connecting capacity to ‘regional distributor’ roads. Local distributor roads are 
designed to serve traffic generated within a specific area.
20.  Accessed 10 November 2025: https://www.transport.wa.gov.au/getmedia/39DE3A1F-5245-4987-9D2B-4D3B33F466E9/fre_p_fs_revagriregionfreightstrategy_final.pdf
Farm size, agricultural machinery, heavy vehicle 
combinations and train lengths have expanded rapidly 
(but) supply chain infrastructure has not kept pace. This 
includes road networks, rail networks, interfaces such 
as intermodal terminals, and regulatory systems and 
processes. Improving efficiency, safety and productivity 
along supply chains is central to Australia’s ability to 
compete in international markets. Global competition 
is increasing, and without a cost-effective supply chain 
Western Australia’s global competitiveness will fall.
Revitalising Agricultural Region Freight Strategy 
 (WA Government, 2020)

260
9. Regional resilience
Wheatbelt grain rail network
 WA’s grain rail network covers 5,500 km and is categorised into three  
‘tiers’ (Figure 7):
•	 Tier 1 lines: high-volume lines that form the core of the network
•	 Tier 2 lines: lines that are economically viable and competitive with  
road transport 
•	 Tier 3 lines: unviable and uncompetitive lines that are no longer 
operational (closed in June 2014)
About half of the 5,500 km rail network (Tier 1 and Tier 2 lines) is dedicated 
solely to grain transport. 
The Tier 2 lines are the focus of infrastructure upgrades through the 
Agricultural Supply Chain Improvements (ASCI) program (which is the 
implementation phase of the Revitalising Agricultural Region Freight 
Strategy). The ASCI is a $200 million federal and state government initiative 
to upgrade regional freight rail infrastructure in WA’s agricultural areas — 
including the Wheatbelt. Key projects include $68 million to upgrade 11 rail 
sidings at CBH grain receival sites to enable faster grain loading onto trains 
with the upgrades said to halve the time taken to load grain into train wagons. 
About $70 million is being used to investigate the potential recommissioning  
of the Tier 3 rail line in the Narrogin-Wickepin-Kulin area.
WA grain exporters currently compete year-round with emerging lower cost 
northern hemisphere producers. 
Improved supply chain efficiency would help ensure that 
local grain harvests can ship to international customers 
quickly, at peak periods of demand, to secure higher 
prices and export earnings.
More than 70% of respondents surveyed as part of the WA government’s 
Revitalising Agricultural Region Freight Strategy (2020)21 supported increased 
investment in rail infrastructure, including intermodal terminals, and there was 
a strong sentiment towards reinstating the Tier 3 railway lines, which were put 
into care and maintenance in 2014 by the current lessee Arc Infrastructure. Rail 
is the most efficient and productive means of transporting large volumes of 
homogeneous goods, such as grain, over large distances with a 120-wagon grain 
train able to deliver in one trip the same amount of grain as 86 triple truck road 
trains (Figure 8). However, current price discrepancies between road (which is 
heavily subsidised by non-freight users) and rail make transporting grain via 
road in large trucks considerably cheaper even for long journeys.

9. Regional resilience
Road maintenance 
The Wheatbelt’s 42 LGAs spent a collective $158 million on road maintenance 
and improvements in 2023–2024 with about 45% of this coming from federal 
funding, 30% from state funding and 25% from local government budgets.22  
About 70% of the funds were spent on ‘road preservation’ — expenditure that 
either maintains the road asset through repair of potholes or grading  
and/or increases the service potential or life of a road through resealing or 
FIGURE 8 (a)  Relative grain-delivery efficiency of trains vs trucks and (b) rail siding upgrade at one of the 11 rail sidings in the Wheatbelt and Great Southern 
regions being funding via the $200 million Agricultural Supply Chain Improvements program. Rail siding upgrades at Brookton and Konnongorring are already 
complete and work is underway at Cranbrook and Moora. The upgrades will halve the time to load grain onto train wagons and increase the efficiency of 
transporting grain to port.

gravel re-sheeting. Less than 30% was spent on road upgrades such as road or 
bridge widening, or bridge replacement to allow greater traffic capacity.
The Wheatbelt contains more than 320 bridges (36% of the WA total) with 
nearly a third of these wooden and built more than 70 years ago. Less than  
$2 million was spent on bridge preservation across the whole of the Wheatbelt 
in 2023–2024. Ageing, wooden bridges act as ‘fuses’ within the Wheatbelt 
road network by restricting the type of vehicles that can drive on the roads.

WALGA (2025)23 estimate that Wheatbelt road expenditure in 2023–2024 
was less than half that required to maintain the region’s roads at their current 
condition (Figure 9). At a state level, local governments spent about 69% of 
the amount required to maintain their roads in their current condition. The 
metropolitan region spent just under 82% of that required to maintain their 
roads (prior to 2019–2020 this figure was always above 100%). The Pilbara 
region achieved the highest preservation performance in the state of 99% while 
the Wheatbelt (south and north) achieved the lowest performance (Figure 9).
Road maintenance performance also differed widely between Wheatbelt 
LGAs in 2023–2024; ranging from less than 20% of the funding required to 
maintain roads in their current condition through to 70–90% in a few shires 
(Figure 10).

9. Regional resilience
Freight network efficiencies 
Local government revenue is a function of rate payer numbers. At a WA state 
level there are 23 people for every kilometre of roads managed by local 
government. In NSW there are 57 people for every kilometre and in Victoria 
there are 52 people per kilometre. In the Wheatbelt there are just 1.9 people 
per kilometre of local government roads.
The Wheatbelt’s road and rail network underpins the agricultural sector, both 
in terms of supplying production inputs such as lime, fertiliser, herbicide and 
fuel and delivering agricultural commodities to receival points and on to ports 
for export. At a national level, freight and logistics costs for grain producers 
are estimated to account for nearly a third (27.5%) of gross farm production, 
the highest of any agricultural bulk product, with about 34% of these costs 
occurring predominantly on local government roads.24 Sauer (2021)25 used 
these findings to estimate that the costs attributable to transporting freight 
along local government roads in the Wheatbelt were in the order of  
$500 million per year. 
24.  AgriFutures (2019) The Impact of Freight Costs on Australian Farms Publication No. 19-005
25.  https://www.westonia.wa.gov.au/council-meetings/ordinary-meeting/july-ordinary-council-
meeting-2021/49/documents/0721-bulletin-july.pdf
“
“Even small savings (in freight and logistics costs) 
would translate to large benefits for the (agricultural) 
industry — for example, a 10% improvement in 
freight efficiency would result in a $50 million saving 
that could be distributed between all participants 
throughout the supply chain.”25

9. Regional resilience

268
9. Regional resilience
Case study
Strategic approach bolsters road funding  
To counter the disadvantage faced by many small local governments 
in maintaining their road networks, the 42 Wheatbelt LGAs have 
made a commitment to work together to improve freight routes 
across the region. The resultant Wheatbelt Secondary Freight 
Network (WSFN) project is the largest known collaboration between 
local governments in Australia — an innovative approach that could 
be used to address other challenges across the Wheatbelt. 
268
9. Regional resilience
Jointly funded between the 42 Wheatbelt LGAs and the state and federal 
governments, the focus of the collaboration is a network of 80 connecting 
freight routes covering more than 4400 km of roads (Figure 12). The project 
has completed a high-level analysis of the current condition of each road and 
what is needed to make these roads fit for purpose. Many of the roads in the 
Wheatbelt region were not designed for and are unable to safely accommodate 
the increase in size and mass of the vehicles required by industry to remain 
competitive. The upgraded road network will enable these larger, high 
productivity trucks safe and cost-effective access through the Wheatbelt. 
The long-term goal is to obtain funding support for a 
broadly estimated $500 million capital works program 
across 2,850 km of the network over a 10–20 year 
timeframe. 
To date (2025) $187.5 million has been sourced with $82,45 million expended on 
87 upgrades, with numerous other projects currently in development. Upgrades 
include the construction of overtaking lanes, road and bridge upgrades, 
shoulder sealing and road train assembly parking bays. While the focus is on 
providing better connections between agricultural regions and ports to assist 
the agricultural sector, the program will also improve access and safety for 
tourism, mining and other sectors.

9. Regional resilience
269
9. Regional resilience
269
FIGURE 12  Map showing the area covered by the 4,400 km Wheatbelt Secondary Freight Network jointly funded between  
the 42 Wheatbelt LGAs and the state and federal governments. Upgrades to the network will enable high productivity trucks safe and 
cost-effective access through the Wheatbelt and lift the efficiency and effectiveness of the Wheatbelt freight network.
Source: Main Roads Western Australia

270
9. Regional resilience
Case study
Northam freight hub  
Three-trailer RAV (Restricted Access Vehicle) 7 road trains  
(36.5 metres), which carry a significant proportion of WA’s domestic 
freight, are limited to certain roads and cannot enter the Perth area. 
Road trains destined for Perth from the eastern states are currently 
required to break up their loads at the road train assembly area in 
Northam on the outskirts of Perth and then deliver ‘dog run’ loads 
to the city, before returning east with another load. This activity and 
mandatory rest breaks effectively delays the driver and linehaul 
equipment for up to three days at Northam. 
The Wheatbelt Development Commission and Development WA, with 
guidance from the Avon Industrial Park Advisory Committee, have completed 
and funded a study examining the potential for Northam to further develop as 
a freight and logistics hub.
The Northam Freight Hub project aims to facilitate infrastructure investment 
in the Avon sub-region, while offering the prospect of relocating freight-
related industries from the eastern suburbs of Perth to a strategically 
advantageous location in the peri-urban Wheatbelt. The initiative seeks to 
optimise operational efficiency and enhance the overall effectiveness of the 
region’s transportation network.
Interest in developing the freight hub has been driven, in part, by CBH 
Group’s purchase of 100 hectares at the Avon Industrial Park to build the 
Meenaar grain receival site. CBH’s development is supported by $6.5 million 
funding for a rail spur under the Agricultural Supply Chain Improvement 
270
9. Regional resilience
(ASCI) program. Further, private developer Procon Developments is currently 
constructing AvonWest: an integrated logistics and enterprise hub at the 
Yilgarn Avenue road train assembly area.
The proposal creates an opportunity to improve 
east-west road transport efficiency and reduce the 
movement of large vehicle combinations into the Perth 
metropolitan area. It would also increase speed to 
market by up to 48 hours and enable a further 20–25 
interstate crossings per year.
Avon Industrial Park, Northam

9. Regional resilience
271
9. Regional resilience
271
Economic diversification is critical to building 
drought resilience in the Wheatbelt but will 
require quality transport infrastructure and 
logistics to export and import goods across 
and within the vast region. A growing Perth 
population also means the Wheatbelt is 
increasingly being relied upon to facilitate 
freight transport from a wide range of 
interstate and intrastate industry supply 
chains. Investing in Wheatbelt freight 
infrastructure and precincts will improve 
the efficiency of these supply chains and 
encourage new and drought resilient 
economic activity in the Wheatbelt.

272
9. Regional resilience
Social services 
Droughts, climate 
change, industry 
development and 
population dynamics 
are all expected to 
place added pressure on 
service delivery across 
the Wheatbelt. 
The remoteness and population decline of some Wheatbelt towns makes it difficult to attract and sustain 
human services while other towns closer to the coast and Perth are growing with retirees, ‘tree changers’, 
tourists and renewable energy and mining developments — all of which increase demand for social services, 
housing and infrastructure. 
Different levels of government are addressing some of these challenges through various initiatives and 
programs including:
Living Well in the Wheatbelt — Towards 203526 —  (released September 2025) a report and 
implementation plan developed by the Wheatbelt District Leadership Group to outline the vision, priorities 
and actions needed to improve social, health and wellbeing outcomes and human service delivery in the 
Wheatbelt.
Wheatbelt Regional Planning and Infrastructure Framework27 — identifying infrastructure and 
planning initiatives that will help diversify the Wheatbelt economy through a shift to value-adding of primary 
industries, tourism and freight and logistics sectors.
Regional Economic Development Grants (RED Grants)28 — a WA state government $50.85 million 
program over nine years investing in local projects to expand/diversify regional industries and develop 
workforce skills/capacity, increase business productivity and attract new investments.

9. Regional resilience
273
The number of GPs in the Wheatbelt declined from 89 (2021) to 85 in 2023 
and emergency departments activity is increasing year-on-year by an average 
3% — however half of ED activity in the region was triaged at a low urgence 
and could potentially have been seen by a GP or other primary health service. 
Overall, the health and aged care workforce in the Wheatbelt is ageing and 
there has been a far higher usage of agency nurses, allied health and locum 
doctors since 2020–21.29
Some Wheatbelt towns are self-funding attractive packages to attract GPs 
and allied health professionals. Quairading in the Avon sub-region recently 
advertised for a GP offering a base salary of $300,000 (supplemented by 
consulting fees) with all costs associated with running the practice covered 
by the shire and a rent-free four bedroom home. 
The total package is worth in the order of $800,000 per year. Shire president 
Peter Smith is reported to have said of the initiative:
‘If we don’t have a doctor, we won’t have a medical 
clinic. Then we won’t have a hospital, we won’t have a 
chemist and so the demise will begin.’30  
Nearly all participants surveyed as part of the inaugural RRR Network (rural, 
regional and remote) ‘What RRR Women Want’ 2024 survey31 (of which 
nearly 40% lived in the Wheatbelt) cited difficulty in accessing essential 
services regionally — with 60% of participants citing lack of access to health 
services as the most problematic (Figure 13).

Needs Assessment include:

•	 Access to community and residential aged care 
•	 Access to dental services
•	 Community transport — access to appointments

a more diverse regional economy, maintaining 
and growing Wheatbelt populations, and building 
drought and climate change resilience.

276
9. Regional resilience
Case study
Aged care model keeps Wheatbelt residents at home   
Older people in regional Western Australia have long faced 
significant barriers to accessing even basic aged care, largely 
due to limited local services and chronic workforce shortages. 
These challenges are compounded by government policies and 
service delivery models that are primarily designed for urban 
environments, where higher population density and infrastructure 
enable greater efficiency and scale. 
In many cases, the care of older Australians in the regions remains under-
served, forcing families to shoulder the burden of care or resulting in older 
people being displaced from their homes and communities to access the 
services they require as they age and their care needs increase. 
Witnessing firsthand the impact this was having in their own community, the 
Pingelly Community Resource Centre (CRC) worked with local aged groups to 
devise a way for older people to age safely and positively in the community. 
276
9. Regional resilience
The Pingelly Somerset Alliance was formed by merging Pingelly Cottage 
Homes and the Pingelly Frail Aged Hostel group to create a single advocacy 
group that then worked with the CRC to attract funding for a place-based 
aged care model. The Commonwealth Department of Social Services 
expressed interest in the concept and following a cost-benefit-analysis of the 
proposal funding was secured for a pilot program.
Staying in Place is a community-led, place-based aged care model that 
enables older residents to remain in their homes while receiving holistic care 
through local services. 
The initiative currently supports 90 clients and has 25+ part-time support 
workers and eight full-time employees. The total value of the care packages 
provided is about $4 million with most of the funding benefiting the local 
economy. The Pingelly CRC, which coordinates the services, generates about 
$400,000 annually to support the service.
The Pingelly community has gained significant economic and social 
benefit from the program including the creation of additional employment 
opportunities for local people, upskilling of the local workforce with locally 
based training, creation of new businesses to cater to client needs and the 
introduction and expansion of activities designed to help older residents 
maintain physical health, mental wellbeing, and social connection.
There are currently 20 towns in the Wheatbelt coordinating care to their 
residents through the model, catalysed by a ‘kick start program’ funded by 
the State government. 
“
“Staying in Place is a powerful example of what happens 
when communities work together and draw on their local 
assets and relationships to solve local issues.”
Jane Harwood, CEO Linkwest

9. Regional resilience
277
The success of the Staying in Place project in Pingelly 
has enabled LinkWest to secure funding to support the 
expansion of the concept to 10 communities in more 
remote areas of Western Australia.

9. Regional resilience
Natural environment 
resilience 
The Wheatbelt 
Region sits within the 
South West Botanical 
Province of Western 
Australia — one of just 
36 globally-recognised 
biodiversity hotspots. 
At a national level, the Central and Eastern Avon Wheatbelt are recognised as one of Australia’s 15 national 
biodiversity hotspots — containing the threatened eucalypt woodlands and the internationally-recognised 
Ramsar wetland of Toolibin Lake, the largest remaining wetland of its type in south-west Western Australia 
(Figure 14).
Biodiversity hot spots are defined as areas with high species richness and 
high vulnerability. To qualify as a biodiversity hotspot an area must contain at 
least 1,500 vascular plant species and have lost more than 70% of its original 
habitat. Many of the plant and animal species in the Wheatbelt are found 
nowhere else in the world.  
278
Image courtesy: Megan Hele

8. Regional resilience
279
8. Regional resilience
279
9. Regional resilience
279
While widespread clearing of the Wheatbelt’s natural 
vegetation from the early 1900s has resulted in one of 
the most productive cropping regions in Australia, some 
local government areas have less than 5% of original 
native vegetation remaining. Eucalypt woodlands that 
once dominated the region are now restricted to small, 
fragmented remnants on reserves, roadsides and private 
property. About two-thirds of these remnant patches are 
less than one hectare and fewer than one per cent are 
greater than 100 ha (Figure 15 a and b).

freshwater wetland in south-west WA.

9. Regional resilience
282
The remnant Wheatbelt woodlands are home to more than 200 threatened 
animal and plant species including the Carnaby’s cockatoo, chuditch, numbat, 
and malleefowl and are one of just 20 places across Australia prioritised by 
the Federal Government’s Threatened Species Action Plan (2022–2032).32 
Local Wheatbelt communities often develop very personal attachments to 
specific reserves, and use granite outcrops, lookouts, breakaways, woodlands, 
lakes and river pools for recreation.33 
The mass wildflower displays of the unique woodlands and shrublands are a 
major tourist attraction across the Wheatbelt with the region attracting more 
than a million visitors in 2023 spending an estimated $400 million.34
Native vegetation on agricultural land provides important windbreaks, reduces 
erosion and salinity and creates habitat for native fauna — including beneficial 
insects and birds.35

The Regional Natural Resource Management Strategy for the Avon  
River Basin (2013)36 highlights the challenges facing each of the five  
sub-catchments within the region and outlines the action needed to protect 
and enhance existing biodiversity (Figure 16). Despite being released more 
than a decade ago, the strategy’s main recommendations are still relevant — 
underscoring the investment and coordination needed to enhance and protect 
the Wheatbelt’s unique and dispersed natural environment. Placing a true 
value on the region’s natural environment through biodiversity and carbon 
offset schemes could help drive environmental investment.

Covering almost 16 million hectares the Great Western Woodlands are the 
largest remaining area of intact Mediterranean-climate woodland on Earth. 
The goal of the Gondwana Link project is to link the woodlands with remnant 
vegetation to the south near Ravensthorpe and Esperance. Most of the Great 
Western Woodlands area is located immediately east of the Wheatbelt.
Southern Wheatbelt: 
20% remaining bushland, 17% remnant patches >10 ha, limited 
fencing on major river tributary
Enhance, protect and manage important biodiversity refuges, address soil 
acidity issues, prevent further salinity expansion, support land use planning, 
fence/re-vegetate major tributaries

“There’s 760 nature reserves across Wheatbelt but their health and biodiversity is poor. 
We need to activate seed banks through burning programs. Nature reserves are the last 
vestiges of what was here before — we need to improve water quality through riparian 
plantings and collect and propagate plant species for the Wheatbelt. We need to map 
priority reserves and overlay these with road, rail and reserve vegetation corridors.”
Mathew Wilson, Ballardong Aboriginal Corporation

9. Regional resilience
Drought impacts
Drought and the drying climate have impacted, and will continue to impact, 
native vegetation communities across the Wheatbelt. Deep-rooted species like 
salmon gums and eucalypt woodlands are increasingly vulnerable to extended 
dry conditions, which combined with higher fire risk and greater reliance on 
shallow groundwater, can lead to canopy dieback and loss of habitat.
Integrating a range of conservation approaches at 
the regional (ecosystem), community (or landscape) 
and species (or operational) level could help improve 
the survival and resilience of species, communities 
and ecosystems, increase the likelihood of successful 
adaptation and decrease their vulnerability to  
climate change37:
Regional level:  strategies include adding areas of native vegetation to the 
conservation reserve system, preserving and linking vegetation corridors 
and implementing species recovery programs. This is the approach of the 
Gondwana Link project a landscape-scale conservation project in south-
western Australia, which is reconnecting fragmented habitats across a  
1,000 km swathe of land, from the wet forests of the far southwest to the dry 
woodlands bordering the Nullarbor Plain in the east. The Wheatbelt lies to 
the north and west of the project. More than 100 organisations are involved 
in the Gondwana Link, which began in 2002 and focuses on “protecting 
remaining bushland and restoring degraded areas to create a healthy, connected 
ecosystem that is resilient to climate change and supports local biodiversity.” 
Could something similar work in the Wheatbelt Region?
288
Image: courtesy: Peel Harvey Catchment Council
37.	Department of Biodiversity, Conservation and Attractions (2021) Wheatbelt Region parks and reserves 
management plan 95 2021. Department of Biodiversity, Conservation and Attractions, Perth.

Community or landscape level: strategies include improving resilience by 
increasing and refining existing management of pressures such as invasive 
plants and animals, diseases and inappropriate fire regimes. The Western 
Australian Aboriginal Ranger Program provides funding to Aboriginal 
organisations leading ranger programs through the allocation of competitive 
grants.  About $100 million has been allocated to the program since its 
inception in 2017 with an additional $69.5 million committed for four years 
from 2026. The program has funded Aboriginal organisations to employ and 
train rangers and other staff, to undertake activities from seed collecting, 
restoration and weed management through to ecological monitoring, research 
and fire management. 
The Noongar Boodja Ranger Team was established in 2014 to provide 
Aboriginal community members with ongoing work on Boodjar (country) 
across the Wheatbelt. Rangers are trained across multiple skill sets including 
safety, operating equipment, native seed collection, weed control and plant 
identification.
Species or operational level: collecting seed and captive fauna breeding 
programs provide a fall-back mechanism for long-term species survival and 
potential re-introduction projects (see case study following page).

8. Regional resilience
289
9. Regional resilience
289
Image: courtesy: Wheatbelt NRM

290
9. Regional resilience
Case study
Boola Boornap tree nursery   
As the carbon offset market grows and interest in land restoration 
increases, so too has the demand for native seedlings. 
Noongar Land Enterprise (NLE)’s Boola Boornap native tree nursery is 
seeking to address the native seedling shortfall and become a leader in the 
burgeoning restoration industry. NLE’s Boola Boornap (meaning ‘The place of 
many trees’ in Noongar) native tree nursery received $100,000 in funding to 
complete irrigation and seedling infrastructure upgrades through Round 4  
of the RED Grants program.
Since November 2020 Boola Boornap has produced about one million 
seedlings from 50 native plant species. Servicing a range of customers 
involved in land rehabilitation, carbon sequestration and sandalwood 
production, the funding will allow NLE to undertake much-needed upgrades 
to meet growing demand.
The grant will assist with the expansion of the tree nursery, increasing 
production capacity to two million seedlings per year, and allow the 
enterprise to gain accreditation that will enable direct sales to the public. 
Australia’s first not-for-profit, Aboriginal-led grower group, NLE strives to 
facilitate a collective approach to land management enterprise development 
on Noongar land. Seeking to reclaim the relationship between people and 
Country, about 70% of Boola Boornap’s staff are Aboriginal, with most hailing 
from the local Ballardong Noongar community.
290
9. Regional resilience
The upgrades will improve the viability and profitability of the business, 
allowing Boola Boornap to take on more staff as the business grows. Long 
term, it is hoped that Boola Boornap, together with other initiatives, will 
enable NLE to become a self-sustaining organisation and branch out to other 
aspects of the supply chain.
Image: courtesy: Wheatbelt NRM

9. Regional resilience
291
The challenges facing natural resource management across the Wheatbelt 
are extensive and multidimensional, demanding a coordinated, landscape-
scale, whole-of-community response.38 Sites most at risk from the cascading 
impacts of dry seasons, shallow soils, and groundwater depletion need to 
be prioritised and monitored. Landholders, Aboriginal ranger teams, and 
community groups need support to identify and buffer critical remnant 
vegetation—including threatened ecological communities, riparian zones, 
and deep-rooted woodlands such as Salmon Gum and York Gum.

9. Regional resilience
• 	 Modernise existing Wheatbelt sub-regional economic development strategies and growth plans so they 
reflect current economic, environmental and social drivers
• 	 Support delivery on recommendations in the Wheatbelt Economic Diversification study (Bankwest Curtin 
Economic Centre)
• 	 Support LGAs to undertake evidence-based studies into priority infrastructure needs and in progressing project 
ready business case proposals
• 	 Coordinate development of sub-regional infrastructure plans across water, power, housing, transport and 
telecommunications
• 	 Explore feasibility of tax incentives (e.g. FMDs) for small-medium enterprises in areas dependent on agriculture
• 	 Deliver an ‘agile Wheatbelt workforce’ initiative  with industry, education and training providers
• 	 Encourage sharing of emergency response and recovery learning to strengthen regional networks and build 
emergency response capacity
• 	 Bring together First Nation groups, NRM agencies and community groups, grower groups and private investors 
to explore ways to expand the nature-based and land restoration economy

10. Implementing the Plan
295
• 	 The Wheatbelt RDRP process has resulted in 31 priority actions for building drought 
resilience across the Wheatbelt
• 	 The actions cover ‘foundational’-type activities involving data collection and modelling 
work; ‘quick wins’ targeted at solving specific local issues and ‘future pathways’ focussed on 
delivering solutions to region-wide drought resilient issues
Wheatbelt implemention plan 
at a glance:

296
10. Implementing the Plan
Introduction
Implementing the 
Wheatbelt RDRP will 
be underpinned by 
regional collaborations 
and alignment with 
Future Drought Fund 
programs.
The drought resilience actions identified in this plan are the culmination of hundreds of discussions held 
in 2024 and 2025, and were informed through data analysis, expert advice and current state and federal 
policy frameworks. They are a snapshot of needs from a region that has recently achieved several record-
breaking grain production seasons, while also facing multiple infrastructure and capacity challenges to 
manage growth, environmental health and critical service delivery to its residents.
Note that the identified actions also incorporate the interventions identified in the Southern Wheatbelt 
RDRP. The Southern Wheatbelt RDRP is available from the Wheatbelt Development Commission.
The Wheatbelt RDRP stakeholder engagement process resulted in about  
75 resilience-building actions being identified across the region’s 
agricultural, community, water, landscape and economic sectors.
296

10. Implementing the Plan
297
The themes will be used to guide a collaborative approach to advocating for, 
and funding the changes needed, to secure drought resilience across the 
Wheatbelt.
The 75+ actions developed during the stakeholder engagement process were 
refined down to 31 actions by the Wheatbelt RDRP steering committee, using 
the following criteria:
• 	Foundational activities – improve understanding, build 
knowledge or networks and raise awareness to inform quick 
win or future pathways (Table 2).
• 	Quick wins – targeted actions that address specific 
challenges or opportunities and are most likely driven by 
regional stakeholders with ability to leverage existing 
programs (Table 3)
• 	Future pathways – are longer-term actions involving 
multiple partners, and which may depend on completion of 
foundational activities (Table 4)
Alignment of the 
project with building 
drought resilience
Strength and quality 
of partnerships to 
deliver
Impact on resilience 
including scale of 
benefits expected
Ability to build local 
capacity
Addressing resource 
use efficiency
Feasibility – including 
opportunity to secure 
or leverage funding 
and readiness
Geographical impact
The 31 actions (see Table 1) outline the drought resilience focus required 
for the Wheatbelt in the short, medium and long term:

10. Implementing the Plan
Monitoring and 
evaluating the RDRP plan  
The following monitoring 
and evaluation framework 
outlines how progress 
and outcomes from the 
Wheatbelt RDRP will be 
measured, analysed and 
communicated. 
Drought Fund (FDF) Monitoring, Evaluation and Learning (MEL) framework.
DAFF’s MEL framework involves three outcome levels:
• 	 Long-term: Improved resilience and reduced impacts of drought 
• 	 Medium-term: Enhanced capability, planning, networks, and decision-making
• 	 Short-term: Improved knowledge, tools, engagement, and access to resources
The Wheatbelt RDRP M&E framework maps each of the plan’s priority actions to the DAFF outcomes.
The ultimate goal is a Wheatbelt region in which communities, landscapes 
and industries can anticipate, absorb and adapt to drought conditions while 
maintaining wellbeing, economic stability and environmental sustainability.
312

10. Implementing the Plan
313
Key evaluation questions
Evaluation of the Wheatbelt RDRP will be done annually and use key 
evaluation questions or KEQs to guide information gathering and analysis 
and identify insights and lessons.
Long-term questions:
•	
To what extent has the Wheatbelt region strengthened its resilience 
to drought across economic, environmental, social, and institutional 
systems?
•	
How effectively have the actions reduced vulnerabilities identified 
through the Drought Vulnerability Assessment?
Medium-term questions:
•	
Are local governments, agencies, businesses, and communities better 
equipped to plan for and manage drought conditions?
•	
Have governance, partnerships, and cross-regional coordination 
improved?
Short-term questions:
•	
Have activities been delivered as intended?
•	
Has awareness, capability, and access to tools improved?
Indicators of progress and data sources
Indicators are grouped by theme and outcome level (short, medium, long). 
Data will be collected using project logs, passive and active monitoring 
activities, surveys and interviews, statistical data sourced from ABS, 
REMPLAN, and agencies including DPIRD, DWER, Water Corporation and 
DBCA (Table 5). 
“
“A resilient Wheatbelt is one that is prepared for 
drought conditions and has strategies in place to 
identify and attend to areas most at risk.”
Wheatbelt RDRP consultancy feedback

10. Implementing the Plan
316
Monitoring and evaluation framework
Monitoring and evaluation will be repeated every 12-months for five years. 
Year 1 will be used to establish benchmark data for refined assessments 
of drought vulnerability, water security, local capacity and economic 
resilience. Reviews in years 2–3 will be used to assess progress against 
indicators (Table 5), document learnings and refine priorities. Reviews in 
years 4–5 will again assess progress against indicators (Table 5) and assess 
long-term changes in resilience across the Wheatbelt. 
“
“Resilient communities have the skills, social cohesion 
and infrastructure needed to manage climate change.”
Wheatbelt RDRP consultancy feedback
Reporting methods will include annual progress reports; outcome harvesting 
reports; regional learning briefs and a final evaluation report. 
Image courtesy: Shire of Pingelly

10. Implementing the Plan
The Wheatbelt 
Development 
Commission (WDC) 
partnered with the 
Department of Primary 
Industries and Regional 
Development (DPIRD) to 
support the delivery of 
the Southern Wheatbelt 
Regional Drought 
Resilience Plan (SW 
RDRP). The SW RDRP 
encompassed the 
Shires of Dumbleyung, 
Wagin, Lake Grace, 
Kulin and Kondinin.
Stakeholder consultation facilitated the contribution of more than 330 individuals, representing  
150 organisations, to the SW RDRP and Drought Vulnerability Assessment (DVA). The DVA informs and 
should be read in conjunction with the SW RDRP. (Links to both documents provided on page 320). 
The Southern Wheatbelt region’s agricultural sector has a history of 
active engagement in climate adaptation and drought preparedness. It is 
recognised that more can be done to build on existing drought resilience.
The Southern Wheatbelt RDRP has five key themes, with a range of interventions and suggested actions 
identified and listed in detail within the SW RDRP shown in Table 6.

TABLE 6. Southern Wheatbelt RDRP themes, interventions and pathways

320
10. Implementing the Plan
The SW RDRP proposed interventions aligned strongly 
with the Future Drought Fund’s strategic priorities and 
objectives. 
Notably, Southern Wheatbelt stakeholders identified improving water system 
resilience and reliability as the number one priority to build drought resilience 
in the region. There was also a desire to better articulate agricultural systems 
research and development needs and build strengthened connections within and 
across the region. 
The themes and interventions within the Plan provided exposure to a broad range 
of investment opportunities and have an evidence base to support engagement 
with stakeholders on implementation. These themes and interventions will now 
be incorporated into the Wheatbelt Regional Development Plan (Wheatbelt RDRP) 
and be actioned accordingly through future investment and initiatives. 
• 	Regional Drought Resilience Planning Program
• 	Southern Wheatbelt Drought Resilience Plan
• 	Southern Wheatbelt Drought Vulnerability Assessment
